# Issues

**The register of everything open in this repo.** Defects, gaps, and questions
awaiting a ruling. One id per item, stable forever.

**This file is the source of truth for what is open.** Nothing is open unless it
is here. Other documents no longer carry their own open-item lists — they link
here. Long analysis may stay where it was argued (`packages/*/PROJECT_STATE.md`,
`IDEAS/*.md`); the **Detail** column points at it. What must
never happen again is an item existing in exactly one document that nobody reads.

**What belongs here:** shipped behavior that is wrong, a gap that blocks
something, a question that needs a decision. **What does not:** features and
roadmap (`IDEAS/`), settled rulings (`DECISIONS.md`), traps that are correct
behavior you must know about (`CLAUDE.md` § Live hazards), history
(`packages/*/CHANGES.md`).

**Closed items age out to [ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md).**
§ Closed below keeps the recent ones; the archive keeps the rest, verbatim.
Together they hold every id ever issued, and an id is never reused — so a
`FJS-###` named in a comment, a handoff, or a package file resolves in exactly
one of the two files. Search both before concluding an id is unknown.

## Conventions

| | |
| --- | --- |
| **Id** | `FJS-###` for a defect or gap, `FJS-D##` for a decision. **Never reused, never renumbered.** Closing an item moves it to § Closed with its id. |
| **Sev** | `S1` blocker — data loss, a security hole, or cannot ship · `S2` high — silently wrong, costs a day to find · `S3` medium · `S4` low or cosmetic |
| **Status** | `open` — verified open on the date shown · `stale?` — inherited from an older audit and **not re-verified**; probe before acting · `contested` — the recorded diagnosis is doubted, see the note |
| **Verified** | The date somebody last ran it. A `stale?` row's date is when it was *written*, not confirmed. |
| **Blocked by** | A row that cannot start until another closes says `blocked by FJS-###` in its own text, and nowhere else. `fli next` takes it out of the ranking until that row closes and weighs the blocker up. Write it only where the order is real — a row that merely relates to another cites it. |
| **By hand** | A row a headless session cannot finish — a run longer than a session lasts, a check only the owner can make — says `by hand` as its own segment of its links cell. `fli next` keeps it ranked and marks it; `fix:loop` skips it. |

**A row has exactly the columns its table's header names**, and `fli
register:check` fails on anything else (`row-shape`). Both ways of getting it
wrong are silent: a row with FEWER columns is one pasted from the other table,
and the reader infers a shape from the cell count, so it is given a status
nobody wrote and no other rule can see it; a row with MORE has the excess
**dropped when the file is rendered** — in § Closed that is the *How* column, so
the row displays with no citations while they sit in the file. Links belong
inside the last declared column, joined with ` · `, and a `|` that is part of a
sentence is escaped `\|`.

`stale?` is load-bearing. The eight rows inherited from the 2026-07-31 audit
(now `IDEAS/review-coherence.md`) were re-probed on 2026-08-10 and **five of the eight were wrong**:
two were already fixed (conduit's secrets, `admin:generate`), one was never
reproducible (conduit's version skew), and two were right about a defect and
wrong about what it was — sierra's prefetch is not an auth *bypass* but a
cached 401, and caravan's row named three bugs of which two were fixed.
**Treat a `stale?` row as a lead, not a fact** (`VERIFYING.md`).

**The register also goes stale in the closing direction, which nothing here was
watching for.** `FJS-043` was fixed in the tree with a comment describing the
old defect in the past tense, and stayed `open` for eight days because closing
is a second action nobody owed. A row's `Verified` date is evidence about when
somebody last *looked*, not about whether the defect survives — 22 rows carried
`open` at 2026-08-02 when this audit ran, which is the same fiction `stale?`
exists to name, wearing the other label.

| <a id="fjs-1503"></a>FJS-1503 | junction · sierra | **A `cookie()` bearer is read over the WebSocket from the cookie the UPGRADE carried, so `FJS-D340`'s redeem — which sets the cookie after the page has loaded and the socket has opened — is invisible to every socket call, and a cookie cleared after load keeps working over the socket until the tab reloads.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Driven in Chrome**, jazzhr Phase 3 (`.probe/phase3/ws-cookie.ts`, `drive.ts`). The applicant's page posts the link's fragment token to a raw route that `setCookie`s it (D340's shape, the first one built), and `bearerClaim({ from: cookie('jazzhr_link') })` reads it. The browser client had already opened `/ws` at boot — **for a signed-out stranger too**: the scaffold topbar reads *live* with no session, where the scaffold's own `index.mesa` comment says a socket opens only once the client holds a token. `http.ts` copies the upgrade's headers into `ws.data.headers`, which is every frame's `ctx.caller.headers`, so `cookie()` digests the upgrade's cookie for the life of the socket. **Measured, same page, straight after the redeem**: `GET /api/applications` (HTTP, sent before the socket connected) → the applicant's row; `WS find stageChanges` → `[]`, `WS find documents` → `[]`, `WS withdraw applications/2` → **404**. On screen: an application with no history, no documents, and a withdraw button that answers *not found*. After a reload (the upgrade now carries the cookie): history 1 row. **The converse is the security half**: after `POST /portal/leave` clears the cookie, with no reload, HTTP reads **0** rows and the socket still reads **1** — *forget this browser* on a shared machine leaves the open tab reading the application. Revoking the grant server-side does end it (**0**), because `bearerClaim` re-reads the grant row per call. App workaround in jazzhr: reload after redeem and after leave. What could own it: the client not preferring the socket for a caller whose credential is a cookie it cannot see; the transport re-handshaking when a raw route sets or clears a cookie a principal resolver reads; or `cookie()` documented as HTTP-only · [PLAN.md § Q3](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/PLAN.md) · [FJS-D340](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d340) · [FJS-1322](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1322) · blocked by `FJS-D548` |
| <a id="fjs-1485"></a>FJS-1485 | litestone | **Nothing declares a row personal or how long it lives, and a column staff must READ cannot be kept out of the `@@log` trail: an applicant tracker's forget rule, its PII and its report input are all comments, and every applicant's name, email and phone is copied into an append-only store at create.** | open | 2026-09-28 | **Measured** 2026-09-28, *jazzhr stressor* Phase 1, on the discovery draft ([schema.lite § Candidate](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/db/schema.lite#L344)). A stranger's `candidate.create` lands `after: {"name":"Ada Lovelace","email":"ada@example.com","phone":"+15555550100",…}` in `db/audit/auditLogs.jsonl`. **Three halves, one missing idea** — [`IDEAS/compliance-from-the-seed.md`](IDEAS/compliance-from-the-seed.md) items 1–3, still PARTIAL, now with a product: **(1) no word for personal data.** `@pii` redirects to `@encrypted`, which the visibility table defines as *caller does not read* — a recruiter must read the name. **(2) no row for "readable, and not retained".** `@@log` redacts only `@encrypted` / `@guarded` / `@secret` (Invariant 7), and each of those also stops staff reading the column; the visibility table (`catalog.snapshot.md` § The visibility table) has no row whose answer is *the caller reads it and the trail does not keep it*. So the choice for a careful team is: audit the model and retain every applicant's PII for the trail's lifetime, or do not audit it. The trail's own `retention 90d` runs only when something calls it ([FJS-1480](#fjs-1480)). **(3) no word for a record's lifetime.** *A person whose every application was rejected more than 180 days ago is forgotten* has no spelling: no `@retain`, no `@@erase`; `@@expires` HIDES rows past a date (every read filters, `withExpired` drops it) and removes nothing; the database-level `retention` is per database, `createdAt`-only and cannot say *rejected AND older than*. **And the report is the casualty.** `StageChange` (the raw input to *median days in each stage*) is FK'd to the application and cascades with it, and `@from(…, count)` is computed at read time — so erasing a person erases the numbers the product is sold on. Whether the answer is an app-written aggregate, an anonymized tombstone, or a declaration the erase verb reads is jazzhr's Q1, built in its Phase 2 · [schema.lite § Candidate](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/db/schema.lite#L344) · [§ StageChange](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/db/schema.lite#L453) · [PLAN.md § Q1](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/PLAN.md) · [compliance-from-the-seed.md](IDEAS/compliance-from-the-seed.md) · [FJS-1480](#fjs-1480) **Amended 2026-09-29, measured (jazzhr Phase 2: 500 applicants, retention over 288, two subject requests, both spellings).** The report half is answered in app code: an aggregate table (spelling A) and a tombstone (spelling B) each keep *applicants by source per quarter* and *median days per stage* byte-identical across the erasure, where plain `delete()` moves every number (R1 500 → 210). **What app code could not reach is the framework's own stores**, and that is this row now: **(a) the trail gains a copy at erasure.** Every forgotten email is in the trail TWICE: in the create's `after`, and in the erasure's own line, the `delete`'s `before` (delete) or the `update`'s `before` (tombstone): 576 hits for 288 people under both spellings. **(b) the trail re-identifies what was kept, 290/290.** A tombstone keeps its id, and the trail's `candidate.create` line maps that id to name and email; spelling A's unkeyed per-stay rows match exactly one application's `stageAt` pair in the trail's `application.update` snapshots (850/850 spans), whose `candidateId` finishes the join. Coarsened to whole days and ISO weeks: still 264/290. Without the trail, 256/290 tombstoned applications are the only one of their (job, applied day). **(c) the file's freed pages** hold 209 (delete) / 90 (tombstone) of the 288 emails; `PRAGMA secure_delete = ON` through `asSystem().sql` leaves 18 and does not persist on the connection, `VACUUM` leaves 0. No word reaches either. **(d) a queued job's payload** keeps the address of a person rejected this week for caravan's `cleanupAfter` (7 d), with no verb to purge by subject. Plus FJS-1497 (a cascade is unseen by `@@log` and `FileStorage`). **Answer, in [`IDEAS/forgetting.md`](IDEAS/forgetting.md): C**, a per-column declaration the trail and an erase verb read; the retention CLOCK stays app code (a 15-line caravan job, run twice, second run 0) · [PLAN.md § Erasure map](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/PLAN.md) · [FJS-1497](#fjs-1497) **Amended 2026-09-29, measured (jazzhr Phase 3, `.probe/phase3/erase-link.sh`): the link's own trail line names the forgotten applicant, under both spellings.** An applicant with a redeemed link (`bearerClaim` over an `ApplicationGrant`, `subject: 'candidateId'`) corrects her phone; the line is `actorType: 'bearer'`, `actorId` = the grant row (FJS-D342, as ruled), `subjectId` = the candidate. After a subject request forgets her, every door answers as if the link never existed — empty 200s identical to a stranger's, 404 for her file and her withdraw, 410 for her old link identical to a garbage token's, a resend that mails nothing; the grant rows, files and freed pages are gone (tombstone: 0 pages; delete: 1) and the outbox and queue hold only `{"grantId":n}`, because the app mints the token at send time. **The trail keeps her**: the grant's line carries her email in its own `before`/`after` (the model is `@@log`'d), and its `subjectId` joins to the `candidate.create` line ("Eve Erased"). D342 was built so a revoked link is answerable as *which link did this*; after erasure the same two columns are a key to the person. forgetting.md's C covers the snapshot half (`[personal]` in the trail); `subjectId` is a second column C must say something about · blocked by `FJS-D547` |
| <a id="fjs-257"></a>FJS-257 | basecamp · outpost | **The Outpost exists and has never run on a real machine.** Was *a deploy with no Outpost reports six green steps and runs nothing*, and the scope has narrowed twice since. Basecamp's half closed 2026-08-19 — `providers/executor.ts` answers outpost / named stub / refusal, `apps.place` writes the placement three jobs read and nothing wrote, and a digest the machine reports is recorded on `Deployment.builtImage` and addressed by every later step. `packages/outpost` is the process on the other end: the route table basecamp already spoke, every route but liveness signed, build-on-target answering the digest of what it built, and a heartbeat that registers the machine as a Conduit target. basecamp's drive runs the REAL server with a fake docker runner, so the protocol both sides speak is graded rather than restated. **Three clauses of this row went false on 2026-09-08 and are struck**: *nothing installs an Outpost on a server* (cloud-init installs one on a machine basecamp bought, and `servers.issueEnrollment` prints the one command that installs one on a machine somebody else made — `docs/PROVISIONING.md` P2/P5), *there is no `pending → provisioning → installing → ready` walk* (the walk is declared on `@@transitions` and driven end to end by `verify:provision`; `ready` was deleted by [FJS-1021](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1021) and `installing` is what a first heartbeat leaves), and *the fleet shares one secret, so a compromised machine can forge another's check-in* (every machine holds a credential minted at enrollment, inbound and outbound both resolve it per machine, and `OUTPOST_SECRET` is not declared any more — P3/P5). **What remains is one claim, and it is now bigger than the row that carried it**: nothing here has run against a real machine. **The Docker-daemon clause is struck** (2026-09-26): `packages/outpost/test/docker-live.test.js` grades the inspector against a real daemon's own byte counts, read off its API socket, and the first meeting found both of the inspector's readings wrong — Docker's human sizes are decimal and were scaled by 1024, so every disk report read 2–7% fuller than the disk, and `volume prune` takes no `--format`, so the unused-volume sweep exited 125 having removed nothing. That grade covers `system df` and `volume prune`; `build`, `run` and `logs` still meet a daemon first on a fleet machine. **What stays open is the box**: the cloud-init script — the Docker install, the Bun install, the systemd unit and the enrollment exchange — has only ever been `sh -n`-parsed. It is this repo's reading of a vendor's documentation, which is the same limit `verify:stripe` names, and the only thing that closes it is a real account and a real box. Build-once-promote-a-digest is still §b for basecamp and the Outpost: V1 builds on the target, which is honest while the Outpost is co-resident with basecamp and stops being honest at the second machine. **The CLI half of digest addressing is not open and the clause saying so is struck** (2026-09-13): `04-build-api` asks the built image what it is and `06-swap` starts `imageAddress`, falling back to the tag only when the inspect fails | open | 2026-09-08 | [packages/outpost](packages/outpost/) · [executor.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/providers/executor.ts) · [enrollment.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/providers/compute/enrollment.ts) · [PROVISIONING.md](packages/basecamp/docs/PROVISIONING.md) · [IDEAS/deploy-plane.md](IDEAS/deploy-plane.md) · by hand · blocked by `FJS-1397` |

| <a id="fjs-1566"></a>FJS-1566 | portal | **Portal, the search stressor: a page that leaves <head> unclosed is captured as ok with 0 words, because the reader counts head as a skipped element and HTMLRewriter never ends it before <body>, so the whole body is skipped** | open | 2026-09-29 | Found seeding the knight persona (2026-09-29). https://lexi-lambda.github.io/blog/2019/11/05/parse-don-t-validate/ opens <head>, then <meta>/<link>/<script>, then <body> with no </head> (valid HTML: the end tag is optional, and <body> implies it). parsePage on the fetched 44 KB answered 0 words; the same page from <body> on, or the <article> alone, answered 4,080. Both readers share the shape: SKIP lists head and counts depth by skip++ / e.onEndTag(skip--), so an implied end never lowers it. Text: [parse.ts:131](../fjs-prototypes/portal/api/src/domain/pages/parse.ts) (SKIP at :64); copy: [article.ts:107](../fjs-prototypes/portal/api/src/domain/pages/article.ts) (SKIP at :28). The Page is written status ok, text and html null, words 0, so Read later opens an empty copy, the library search never finds the page, and nothing on the page says the capture failed. A fixed parser will not reach these rows by itself: capture.ts:71 counts words != null as having a copy, so a link check never recaptures a 0-word page; staleParses (reparse from snapshot) would, if PARSER is bumped. Fix: treat <body> (or the first body-only element) as the end of head, or drop head from SKIP and skip only its children (title, meta, link, script, style are already skipped or empty) |
| <a id="fjs-1565"></a>FJS-1565 | litestone | **createTenantRegistry drops an option it does not know without a word, so a caller passing plugins at the top level gets a registry with no plugin in it** | open | 2026-09-29 | Found building FJS-1560's first half: litestone repl and export passed plugins beside clientOptions, so under strategy database --level and --gate never reached a tenant client. | <a id="fjs-1517"></a>FJS-1517 | litestone · sierra | **A `@capability` column is charged to its grant whenever the payload NAMES it, `null` and unchanged included, and a generated create form names every column `make()` seeded — so on a model with one, the generated create form is refused for everybody who does not hold that column's grant, for a field the form does not even show.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Driven in Chrome** (jazzhr Phase 4, `.probe/phase4/drive.ts` step `draft-offer`): the recruiter's generated offer form, `<Form resource={offers} only={['title','salary','startOn','terms']}>` over `offers.make({ applicationId, jobId })`, answers *"Offer.signedName" requires the capability "Offer.signedName"* on submit, and no offer is written. `Offer.signedName` is `@capability` so that only the candidate's link may sign (the grid's column tier, the one spelling for *may sign and nothing else*). **Measured on a bare `$setAuth`** (`.probe/phase4/capability-null.ts`), a caller holding `Offer.create` + `Offer.update`: create without the key → ok; create with `signedName: null` → refused; update `{ title, signedName: null }` on a row whose `signedName` is already null → refused. Two halves: litestone's `_checkUpdate` (and the create path) grades a `@capability` key by presence, where *writing* the column is what the grant is about and a null create or an unchanged value writes nothing; and sierra's create posts the whole `make()` record, `only` narrowing what is SHOWN and not what is SENT (its patch sends only changed keys, which is why an edit form on the same model works). | <a id="fjs-1516"></a>FJS-1516 | litestone | **On a `@@capabilities` model, a `@system` column the APPLICATION names on a write (`system: ['respondedAt']`) is charged to the CALLER's `Model.update` grant, so a principal who holds only a move cannot have the app stamp anything beside it: the candidate's accept, which the app timestamps, is a 403 asking the candidate for `Offer.update`.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Measured over HTTP** (jazzhr Phase 4, `.probe/phase4/offer.sh` step 27: the link holder's `accept` → 403 *"Offer.update" requires the capability "Offer.update"*) **and on a bare `$setAuth`** (`.probe/phase4/system-grid.ts`): a bearer holding `Offer.accept`, `Offer.decline` and `Offer.signedName` may `update({ data: { status: 'accepted', signedName } })` and may `update({ data: { status: 'declined' } })`, and the same two writes plus `respondedAt` with `system: ['respondedAt']` are each refused for `Offer.update`. On a model with no grid the same shape passes (`Candidate`: `phone` plus `forgottenAt` with `system: ['forgottenAt']`). Cause, read: `CapabilityPlugin._checkUpdate` (`plugins/capability.js:87`) sorts payload keys into `@capability` columns, move fields and *ungraded*, and charges any ungraded key to `Model.update`; it never reads `args.system`, so a column the app supplies counts as one the caller edits. `@system` means *the application writes it, not the caller* (litestone AGENTS.md § access words), and naming it on the call is how the app says so, which the gate and the column rules honour and the grid does not. The ways around it are each worse: hand the stranger `Offer.update`, or write the stamp as a second `asSystem()` write, which is a second trail line (measured over HTTP: `actorType: 'system'`, still `actorId` = the grant and `subjectId` = the candidate) and splits what should be one line binding the move, the sealed terms, the signature and the time. jazzhr takes the second · [capability.js `_checkUpdate`](packages/litestone/src/plugins/capability.js) · [FJS-1245](#fjs-1245) · [jazzhr offers.service.ts](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/api/src/services/offers.service.ts) |

| <a id="fjs-1513"></a>FJS-1513 | litestone · cli | **`migrate dev` writes a migration it marks BLOCKED (a NOT NULL column with no default, its rebuild commented out), then APPLIES it and records it applied, so the dev database is half-migrated and the file can no longer be fixed in place; the next run blames `db push`, which nobody ran, and advises `litestone db reset`, which is not a command.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Measured**, jazzhr Phase 4 (`fli db:migrate`, which runs `litestone migrate dev`). Adding `jobId Int @immutable` + relation to `Offer` (one table rebuild beside another for `Interview`): the file `20260929043234_migration.sql` carries `-- "offer": rebuild BLOCKED — adds NOT NULL column(s) with no DEFAULT` with the rebuild commented out — a good refusal, with the three ways out — and in the same command it is applied (`✓ 20260929043234_migration.sql (11ms)`: the Interview rebuild ran, the Offer one did not), recorded in `_litestone_migrations`, and followed by *the migration history does not build the schema this app declares*. Hand-writing the Offer copy into the file, as the file itself advises, then changes nothing: the file is applied, `migrate status` reports *modified (checksum mismatch — edited after apply)*, and `migrate dev` now says *`db push` writes tables and no file, so a pushed change lives here* — the same wrong blame as [FJS-1490](#fjs-1490), from a different cause — and offers *Start clean: `litestone db reset`*, which answers *Unknown db subcommand "reset". Available: push* (the string exists only in that advice, `tools/cli.js:901`). jazzhr's dev database was empty, so it was deleted and the history replayed (6 applied, clean). On a database with rows, the way out is by hand. Expected: a file the generator marks blocked is written and NOT applied, and the command exits naming the file to edit · [cli.js:901](packages/litestone/src/tools/cli.js) · [FJS-1490](#fjs-1490) · [jazzhr migration](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/db/migrations/main/20260929043234_migration.sql) |
| <a id="fjs-1512"></a>FJS-1512 | litestone | **A sealing model has one freeze instant, the seal, so a column that must be written AT a later move and frozen from then — a signature, written by the accept after the terms were sealed at approve — has no word: `@immutable` freezes it before it can be written, a second `@seals` is refused, and the nearest spelling leaves the signature rewritable after acceptance.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Measured**, jazzhr Phase 4 on a bare `$setAuth` (`.probe/phase4/sign.ts`, `sign-once.ts`). `Offer` is `approve: draft -> approved @seals` over `@immutable` terms (the seal must be at approve: sealing at accept leaves the terms editable while `sent`, Phase 0), and `signedName String? @capability`, which only the candidate's link holds. (1) **As declared**, signing and accepting are two writes, a signed offer can be declined (`declined`, `signedName: 'Ada Four'`), an accept needs no signature, and after `accepted` the link holder rewrites the signature (`'Ada One'` → `'Somebody Else'`, 200). (2) **`signedName @immutable`**: refused at `sent` with `SealedDocumentError` — the column froze at approve, before anybody could sign — and the one-write sign+accept is refused as *"Offer.accept" was refused by an @@allow/@@deny policy on update*, which names a POLICY for what is the seal's refusal. `fli db:explain @immutable` says *written once, at create, and never again by anybody*, which is not what it does on a sealing model. (3) **`accept: sent -> accepted @seals` beside approve's**: refused at parse (*already sealed by 'approve' … seals nothing*), correctly for what `@seals` means. (4) **What jazzhr ships**: one write carrying `status: 'accepted'` + `signedName` (graded as the `accept` move plus the `Offer.signedName` grant; the trail line has `transition: 'accept'`, the sealed terms and the name in one `after`), `@@check("(status = 'accepted') = (signedName IS NOT NULL)")` (refuses sign-alone, unsigned accept, sign+decline), and `@allow('write', status == 'sent')` on the field, which turns a later rewrite by the link holder into a **silent 200 no-op** — and `asSystem()`, a job or `fli tinker` still rewrites it (`'Mallory'`, measured). A candidate spelling: a move-scoped freeze, e.g. `signedName String? @immutable(after: accept)` or `@sealedBy(accept)`, frozen from the named move's target the way `@immutable` is from the seal · [jazzhr schema.lite `Offer`](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/db/schema.lite) · [seal.js](packages/litestone/src/core/seal.js) · [FJS-1482](#fjs-1482) · [PLAN.md § Q4](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/PLAN.md) |

| <a id="fjs-1508"></a>FJS-1508 | sierra | **`resource.transitions(row, level)` grades the gate, the policy and `@system`, and not `@@capabilities` — sierra has no reference to the grid at all — so on a model whose moves are said by the grid, the button list offers every move to everyone, and the server refuses each by name.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Driven in Chrome**, jazzhr Phase 3 (`.probe/phase3/drive.ts`, step `transitions-offered`). `Application` is `@@gate("0")` + `@@capabilities`, and the applicant's link holds exactly `Application.withdraw` (the grant row's `capabilities`, emitted as `auth().capabilities` — the only spelling for *the bearer may only withdraw*, FJS-1245 / FJS-1486). `applications.transitions({ stage: 'applied' }, 0)` in the applicant's page → `screen:true, interview:true, reject:true, withdraw:true`; over HTTP the same caller's `reject` is **403** *"Application.reject" requires the capability*. `transitionsAt` (`sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js`) asks `@system` and the gate; FJS-495 added the policy half in litestone; `rg capabilit packages/sierra/src` is empty. So the browser's list misses the grid AND the post-update policy, where the server-side one knows both. jazzhr cuts the list by `session.user.capabilities` by hand ([`_Offers.mesa`](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/web/src/routes/pipeline/_Offers.mesa)) and lets the post-update refusal show as an alert; it cannot cut by the policy without restating it |
| <a id="fjs-1507"></a>FJS-1507 | toolbelt · ui | **`make()` seeds a `File` column with `{}`, and `FileField` reads any non-null value that is neither a URL nor a picked `File` as an unresolved stored reference — so every blank create form with a `File` column opens with a red *"This file reference was not resolved into a URL, so it cannot be shown"* before the person has touched it.** | open | 2026-09-29 | **Driven in Chrome**, jazzhr Phase 3 (`.probe/phase3/drive.ts`, step `upload-form-blank`): `documents.make({ applicationId: 1 })` → `{"applicationId":1,"kind":"resume","file":{}}`, and the applicant's *add a document* form renders `[data-file-unresolved]` (1) in `text-danger` on first paint. Cause: the column's JSON Schema is `$ref: FileRef`, which resolves to `type: object`; `makeFromSchema` seeds `TYPE_DEFAULTS.object` (`{}`), cloning it per instance (`toolbelt/src/jsonschema/jsonschema.js`, `make()`); `FileField`'s `unresolved = value != null && !existing && !picked` is then true. The kit's own comment says a raw ref reaching a form is *not the caller's fault* and is reported — here nothing was read at all. A `File` column should seed `undefined` (FileField's *keep* value), the way a `date-time` already does · [FJS-409](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-409) · [PLAN.md § Q3](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/PLAN.md) |

| <a id="fjs-1501"></a>FJS-1501 | mesa | **A prop whose default reads a plain `const` declared above it compiles clean and throws at render: `const a = 'x'` then `export let b = a` is *Cannot access 'a' before initialization*. | <a id="fjs-1494"></a>FJS-1494 | sierra | **`autoImport` has no precedence: a name found twice is a build error. A site cut from a template needs the client's block to win over the template's, and ksite's tag resolver is exactly that order — page-local `_X.md`, then `content/blocks/X.md`, then `content/blocks/X.svelte`, then the engine's `src/blocks/X.svelte`.** | open | 2026-09-28 | **Measured** 2026-09-28, *ksite stressor* Phase 1, by reading ksite's `~/code/KOBAMI/SITES/ksite/site/src/scripts/resolve-component.js` against [auto-import-plugin.js `claim`](packages/sierra/src/build/auto-import-plugin.js#L100), which throws *Auto-import naming conflict*. This row is about TAGS. Layout names are their own namespace since [FJS-1493](#fjs-1493), with an ordered `markdownLayouts` list where a later directory wins, so `Trust.md` saying `layout: Trust` no longer collides with anything. The cost to the port is that ksite's single `src/blocks/` has to split into `src/blocks/` (tags) and `src/layouts/` (wrappers). Two tag cases have no spelling yet. **(a)** A client block overriding the template's, which is the seam the ksite stressor's Q5 turns on. **(b)** The page-local tier: `_Hero.md` beside a page, meant for that page alone. A co-located `_x.mesa` is never routed, and it is never auto-imported either. The ordered list `markdownLayouts` uses is one candidate for (a). |
| <a id="fjs-1495"></a>FJS-1495 | mesa | **An unresolved `<Hero />` compiles clean and fails the prerender with *`Hero` does not exist during a server render — a build has no browser. In dev the same file compiles to `Hero($$el0, …)` against an undeclared identifier, and so does Hero's own `<Image />` and `<LeadButton />`. The compiler has the file's whole binding set when it emits a component call, so a tag naming nothing in scope can be a compile error that names the tag and the two ways to bind it (an import or `autoImport`). |

| <a id="fjs-1474"></a>FJS-1474 | litestone | **The docs send a data migration to a `.js` file, and one `.js` file anywhere in the history makes `migrate create` refuse for the rest of the app's life. [`docs/migrations.md`](packages/litestone/docs/migrations.md) § *JS migrations*: *For data migrations — backfills, transformations, seeding — create `.js` files*. `buildShadow` answers `js-migrations` for any history holding one ([migrations.js:320, 385–390](packages/litestone/src/core/migrations.js)), since a `.js` file can change the schema through `sys.sql` and cannot be replayed. So after a one-column schema change `migrate create` prints *…no migration can be derived from it. Write this one by hand* under `✓`, **exit 0**, with the `.js` file still only PENDING. Read, not run: `historyGap` and `migrate check` then answer *unknown*, which turns off the deploy's history guard. **The two ways out are both rulings.** Either a data change is not a migration, and the docs name where it goes instead (`seed run` executes raw SQL with foreign keys off; `loadFixture` never deletes; both read, not run). Or the shadow learns to skip a `.js` file that declares it touches rows only. Remnant chose neither for its corpus correction: an app script chained after `migrate apply` in `db:migrate`, idempotent by the rows it expects (`remnant/PLAN.md` Q2). **The ✓ and exit 0 on a refusal is a plain defect either way**: a script reading the exit code is told a migration was written. Reproduced alone against the app's Vite 8.3.1: watching the file gives `change` on a write, `unlink` on a remove, then NOTHING for the recreate, a later write or a rename-over. Watching its DIRECTORY gives `unlink`, `add`, then `change` for every later write, so the fix is both halves: watch `dirname(schemaPath)` and regenerate on `add` as well as `change`, filtered by path. | <a id="fjs-1466"></a>FJS-1466 | litestone · junction | **`@@fts` has no way to say *match without the marks*. None of its tokenizers folds Greek accents or Hebrew points, and `tokenize:` takes a name and nothing else. So the fold is app code on both sides, a loader that writes a shadow column and a service that folds the query, and the declared `$search` directive, which does neither, answers `βίβλος` as typed with 0 rows.** | open | 2026-09-28 | **Measured** 2026-09-28, *remnant, the legacy-port stressor*, Q3, on the real corpus (31,100 verses, 443,808 words). **Every existing route, one at a time:** (1) the four tokenizers: 0 of 6 probe words (`γενεσεως βιβλος Ιησου בראשית ברא אלהים`) against the text as written. Under the default `unicode61`, Hebrew points are separators, so `בְּרֵאשִׁ֖ית` indexes as the fragments `ב`, `אש`, `ית`. (2) A tokenizer argument: `tokenize: "unicode61 remove_diacritics 2"` is refused at parse (`Expected IDENT`), and it would not help: over NFD text in plain FTS5 it folds 2 of 6, because FTS5's diacritic table stops at U+0331, so the perispomeni (U+0342), the iota subscript (U+0345) and every Hebrew point and accent (U+0591–U+05C7) survive. (3) **The nearest declaration that exists**: `function fold(s: String): String { @@expr("replace(…)") }` plus `textFolded String @fold(text)`. It takes 199 nested `replace()`s (the 147 precomposed letters and 52 marks this corpus holds, 3.7 KB of SQL), and it agrees with the JS fold on all 443,808 words and hits 6 of 6. But it writes in 24.1 s against 8.8 s for a JS-folded column (9.0 s for `@@fts` over the raw text), it is keyed to this dataset's characters (a data release bringing one more is silently unfolded), and the query side still needs a JS twin: a schema function is inlined into DDL, not a callable SQL function, and bun:sqlite registers none. **What remnant shipped** ([fold.ts](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/api/src/core/fold.ts): NFD, strip `\p{M}`, drop transliteration's syllable dots): five shadow columns written by the loader, and the query folded in [search.service.ts](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/api/src/services/search.service.ts). Over HTTP every probe and all four of Q3's queries (`G0976`, `βίβλος`, `biblos`, `record`) find Matthew 1:1, median 1–8 ms. **What it costs:** nothing ties the shadow to its source, so a write of `text` that forgets `textFolded` leaves the index answering the old text. Adding it to a loaded corpus took a 46 s refold through the client, because SQL cannot compute the fold, while the same fold at load time is free. And **every other entrance misses**: `GET /api/verses?$search=βίβλος` → 0 rows, `biblos` → 0, and only a pre-folded `βιβλος` → Matthew 1:1. **And it is not only accents**: the corpus writes ί as U+1F77 in all 2,138 lemmas that carry it, while a Greek keyboard types U+03AF, which is canonically the same letter in different bytes. So an unnormalized index misses even a correctly accented query. **Candidate**: a word on the index, `@@fts([text], fold: marks)` (NFD, strip marks), with litestone keeping the shadow on every client write, as it already stamps `@default(ulid())` in JS, and folding the query inside `search()`. Then `$search`, a Resource's search and a console all agree. Measured for Greek and Hebrew only; by FTS5's own table the same holds for any mark above U+0331 (read, not measured) · [remnant PLAN.md Q3](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/PLAN.md) · [remnant search.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/api/test/search.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-1464"></a>FJS-1464 | litestone | **`contains`, `startsWith` and `endsWith` pass the caller's `%` and `_` to `LIKE` as wildcards. `contains: '_'` matches all 31,100 verses, none of which holds an underscore, and `startsWith: 'j_'` matches 4,842.** | open | 2026-09-28 | **Measured** 2026-09-28, *remnant, the legacy-port stressor*, Phase 2, through the client (`$setAuth(null).verse.count`) on the loaded corpus: `english contains "_"` → 31,100 (a scan of the rows finds 0 holding `_`), `contains "%"` → 31,100, `startsWith "%"` → 31,100, `reference startsWith "j_"` → 4,842 (every John, Jude, Judges, Joshua, Job…; a literal `j_` prefix matches 0), `contains "gene%logy"` → 10, the same as `genealogy`. **Cause**: [query.js:514](packages/litestone/src/core/query.js) (and the JSON-path twin at :795) builds `params.push(\`%${operand}%\`)` with no `ESCAPE` clause and no escaping of `%`, `_` or `\`. **Why it matters**: `contains` is what a search box reaches when a model has no `@@fts`, and what a filter form's text field sends, so a person's `50%`, `snake_case` or `C_1` answers rows that don't hold it, and the result looks plausible. It also hides the port trap the other way: the old FJS line added the `%` by hand (`contains: '%' + term + '%'`, remnant's old Search), and today that doubles harmlessly (10 = 10), so a porter never learns the operator already wraps. **Only the client layer was measured**; the HTTP filter reaches the same builder by reading, not by a request. Fix: escape `\` `%` `_` in the operand and emit `LIKE ? ESCAPE '\'`, the one place for all three operators; a caller who wants a pattern has `$raw` · [remnant PLAN.md Q3](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/PLAN.md) |

| <a id="fjs-1458"></a>FJS-1458 | auth | **Under `tenancy { strategy row }`, `POST /auth/register` makes a person who can reach nothing, and auth offers no step at which the app could give them a tenant. `onRegister` runs before the row exists and may only refuse; `createUser` writes `email`, `name` and `role` and nothing else, so the tenant column cannot be passed through it** | open | 2026-09-28 | **Measured** 2026-09-28, *remnant, the legacy-port stressor*: `User` scoped by `accountId`, auth's `sessionFields` untouched (auth copies `user.accountId` onto the session itself). `POST /api/auth/register` answers 200 and a token; the row holds `accountId NULL`; `GET /api/users` with that token answers 403 *This session carries no 'accountId'…*. And a live store refuses a frame that is not a row. So the only spelling that paints a result as it arrives, into a live store and through the pipeline, is to write the result as rows, which for *Portal, the search stressor* is keeping the query.** **Measured** 2026-09-27 (PLAN.md Q2), five fake providers (40 ms, 40 ms, 600 ms, flaky, dead), the page painting each as it lands. Three spellings built and driven at the API (10 rounds) and in Chrome (5 rounds). **A**, a raw route over `ctx.sse()`, with the metered half through a service call: first hit ≈ 50 ms. It streams only after working around `FJS-1416`. It skips the gate, hooks, directives and the envelope, and it is HTTP only, beside the page's open socket. No browser package reads `text/event-stream`, and `EventSource` cannot carry a bearer token, so the browser half is fetch, a reader and the frame format by hand (≈ 40 lines). It is the one spelling that reaches only the connection that asked, and the one that hears a hang-up (`onDisconnect`). **B**, each merged hit a `SearchHit` row in a live `list()`: first hit ≈ 60 ms, 46 announcements and 20 rows per search, each row holding the query in its title and URL, on disk until swept (`@@expires` schedules nothing). Every socket the person has open received every row (462 of 462). Ranking is frozen at write time, and it exposed `FJS-1419`. **C**, `searchFrames` answering once the first provider lands and the rest sent as `usages frame` on `search:user:<id>`: first hit ≈ 46 ms. Every frame reached the person's other tab, query included (41 of 41 at the API, and a second Chrome tab refused tab one's frames with *ignored an update with no id*). Every live `Usage` store refuses every frame with that warning, so a non-row payload can land in no store. **The fork, written down:** stream outside the pipeline (A), or write to stream (B). C is neither: its frames leave the pipeline after the first. The same ask from another side as `FJS-1403`, `FJS-1410` and `FJS-1412`: a method's answer has no declaration, so it cannot say it arrives in parts or that it must not be kept. Candidate shapes, not coined: a method that answers in frames over the calling connection, each through `protect()`, with the client exposing them to a store as a sequence of one result; or a connection on the service context, so an after-return frame can at least be addressed | open | 2026-09-27 | [portal PLAN.md Q2](../fjs-prototypes/portal/PLAN.md) · [portal fakes/progressive.ts](../fjs-prototypes/portal/fakes/progressive.ts) · [portal fakes/drive-web.ts](../fjs-prototypes/portal/fakes/drive-web.ts) · [DECISIONS.md FJS-D13](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d13) · `FJS-1416` · `FJS-1419` · `FJS-1403` · `FJS-1410` · `FJS-706` |
| <a id="fjs-1413"></a>FJS-1413 | conduit · junction | **Conduit's default trace hands every target the inbound request's correlation id (`X-Request-Id`, and the same id as `traceparent`'s trace id). Junction writes that id on every audit row of the request, so a rented provider's log of a query can be joined to the person who typed it.** The default exists on purpose (`FJS-742`: a target's logs could not be joined to the request that caused them), and between two services one team owns that is right. Sent to a stranger that holds the query, it is the join key. **Measured** 2026-09-27 in *Portal, the search stressor*, through the real API. The fake provider received `q=portal-canary-7f3e-http` with `x-request-id: 9d818898-…` and `traceparent: 00-9d818898…-…-01`. Our response carried the same id, and so did our audit row for that request, `user update accountId` (the account a first search makes), beside the user's id and IP. So a provider can learn who searched what on any request that writes an audited row. In Portal that is the first search. In an app whose search stamps anything `@@log`'d (a `lastSearchedAt`) it is every search. The request log would carry the id on every search too, and does not only because of `FJS-1414`. A client's own `X-Request-Id` is forwarded verbatim. The switch is conduit-wide: `trace: () => null` sends neither header, to any target (pinned). The hook receives the request, so it could decide per target, but the plugin's default (`junctionTrace`) is not exported. Keeping traces to one's own services while dropping them to providers therefore means rewriting it. The fingerprint stored beside the entry is a SHA-256 of the body, which for a short query is a dictionary lookup away from the query. | <a id="fjs-1409"></a>FJS-1409 | conduit | **A request's own `timeout_ms` is graded as the target's fault, so one impatient caller opens a healthy target's breaker for every caller of that conduit.** `timeout` is in `TARGET_FAULTS` whichever timer produced it: the target's declared `timeout_ms`, or a shorter one the CALLER put on the request. **Measured** 2026-09-27 in *Portal, the search stressor*. A target declares no timeout, so its 600 ms answer is fine by its own policy. Five sends with `timeout_ms: 100` give five `timeout`s, and the next send, with `timeout_ms: 2000`, answers `circuit_open` (control: the same send on a fresh conduit answered in 600 ms). In the app, a 400 ms search budget sheds the `slow` provider after five queries, and a page that would wait two seconds for it (Phase 4's progressive results) is told `circuit_open … retrying in 24s`. The terminal names a healthy provider *circuit_open*. For the 400 ms caller the skip is what it wants, because a request it will always abandon is spend with no answer, and that is exactly why the skip belongs to that caller: the breaker is per target, the patience is per caller, and conduit cannot tell them apart. Candidate: a timeout shorter than the target's own counts as `other` (trips nothing, clears nothing), or the breaker keys on the target and the caller's timeout together. **Measured beside it, and right**: the breaker does count a budget miss, so `dead` is asked 5 times in 50 and skipped from the sixth, and `flaky` at 100 % likewise; at 30 % it never trips (15 misses in 50, never five in a row). A 20-query burst asks `dead` 20 times before the first fault lands, since the breaker counts completed faults; that is ordinary breaker behavior, bounded by `max_concurrent` **Phase 4, on the page (2026-09-27)**: the progressive page waits 2 s per provider (every target's ceiling) and the terminal sends its 400 ms as each request's `timeout_ms`, over one shared conduit. Phase 3 measures each of the four stores with a canary; this row is the *cannot say*, that one will be the *what leaks*. Sibling of `FJS-1247` (`@@anonymous`, the other absence that used to be a comment) **Phase 3 (2026-09-27), the *what leaks*, measured with a canary through the terminal, HTTP and WS** ([`fakes/canary.ts`](../fjs-prototypes/portal/fakes/canary.ts), the real API against a throwaway database). **Clean:** all 14 tables, both SQLite files' raw bytes (live and after stop), the `@@log` trail, the request log for HTTP and WS, stderr, `/metrics`, `fli`'s own state and temp files, and every other file written during the run. A second socket heard no frame with the canary, and the counter moved 5 for 5 answered searches (0 for a replay). **Of the four defaults, one does nothing**: the service cache hooks only `find` and `get`, so `cache: true` on the search service changes nothing (two identical searches both reached every provider). **One is load-bearing**: a `channel: false` service's custom method still announces its answer on the bus, so without `$.dispatch = false` the query is published. **Two stores this row did not list:** `FJS-1412` (an `Idempotency-Key` keeps the answer, query and all, for 24 h, and no method can refuse it) and `FJS-1413` (each provider is handed the correlation id our audit rows carry, so its copy of the query joins to a person). The terminal adds a sixth, app-side: `fli portal:search <q>` puts the query and the email in shell history, and its `/` prompt does not. **So *never keep* today is six decisions:** `$.dispatch = false`, no model and so no `@@log`, no `q` in any error (junction's refusals quote none, measured), idempotency off for the whole app, conduit's trace rewritten for the whole conduit, and a query never taken as an argument. **What was wanted is one word on the method's input**, read by idempotency, the announcement, the logger, conduit's trace and `fli check`. | <a id="fjs-1318"></a>FJS-1318 | ui | **`CommandPalette` cannot show a server's search results: it takes no query callback, and it ranks whatever `items` it is given against the typed words AGAIN, so a hit made on text the row does not show is dropped and the person reads *No results*.** | open | 2026-09-23 | **Driven in a real browser**, linear Phase 6 (Chrome, `vite dev`). linear's ⌘K is fed by `GET /api/search` ([search.service.ts](../fjs-prototypes/linear/api/src/services/search.service.ts): FTS5 over issues, comments and projects, graded per model). So [linear `SearchPalette.mesa`](../fjs-prototypes/linear/web/src/components/SearchPalette.mesa) finds the palette's input in the DOM by its private class (`.fjs-cp-input`), listens for `input`, asks the server and replaces `items`. The palette then passes those items through `rank(items, query, { keys: ['label', 'group', 'sub'] })` and draws only what that keeps. **Measured:** *throttling*, a word in ENG-3's description: the server answered **1** hit (`Rate-limit the search endpoint`), and the palette drew **0 rows**, *No results for "throttling"*. *flamingo* (inside a comment's first 80 characters) and *load-test* (in a title) drew 2 and 1, because the word happens to be in the label. So the palette works for exactly the hits that did not need a server. The same filter also hides every static command (*Go to …*) while a server query is open, and it re-orders the server's bm25 ranking by the device's fuzzy score. Worth: an async source, `search={(q, signal) => Promise<items>}`, whose answer is drawn as given, ranked by whoever produced it. Or at minimum an `onquery` callback and a `filter={false}` · [CommandPalette.mesa](packages/ui/components/overlay/CommandPalette.mesa) |

| <a id="fjs-1291"></a>FJS-1291 | litestone | **A row policy cannot say *the caller is one of this row's members*. In linear that is the rule for a private team, for which people you can see, and for which workspaces you are in. The only spelling today computes the rule in the principal resolver.** | open | 2026-09-27 | [FJS-D221](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d221) built one-hop `relation.column` and deferred *any child matches* as the uncommon case: *not being ruled here and should not be pre-paid for with syntax*. linear's Phase 1 is the measurement D221 asked for, with three sites in one schema: `Team` (a private team is visible to its `TeamMember`s), `User` (someone who shares a workspace with me, through `Membership`) and `Workspace` (every workspace I am in, for the switcher). **What linear wrote:** a bare `claim teamIds`, filled per request by a resolver that composes `membershipClaim` and reads `TeamMember` through `asSystem()` ([principal.ts](../fjs-prototypes/linear/api/src/core/principal.ts)), and `@@allow('read', (private == false && auth().memberRole != 'guest') \|\| id in auth().teamIds)`. **Measured, and it works:** a SEC member sees `[ENG, SEC]` and a non-member `[ENG]`. The non-member sees neither SEC's issues nor its comments (`check(team)` → `check(issue)`), a guest sees `[]`, and another workspace's admin sees `[]`. Over HTTP, a member not in the private `DES` gets `['ENG', 'SEC']`. **The cost is where the rule lives.** Which teams a person is in is decided in TypeScript and trusted by every policy, the shape AGENTS.md says access must never take. A job, a seed or `fli tinker` has to recompute the list or grade wrong, and the unit tests pass a hand-written list, so they never run the resolver (linear's first filter bug in it surfaced only over HTTP). The list also rides on every principal into every compiled `IN`. `User` and `Workspace` took the tight answer instead: your own row, with the people picker reading a `displayName` copied onto `Membership`, which is a second origin for one fact, the thing D221 exists to remove. The candidate spelling is `members.any(userId == auth().id)`, compiled as a correlated `EXISTS` at the one-hop cost D221 already accepted. Whether that or the resolver is the road is a ruling, not a fix **Amended 2026-09-23, linear Phase 6 (browser): three costs on screens.** (1) **The workspace switcher cannot name the workspaces it switches to.** `GET /memberships?$populate=workspace` answers `workspace: null` for every workspace but the current one, because `Workspace` reads `id == auth().workspaceId`. linear names each one with a hand-written `fetch` carrying that workspace's own `X-Workspace-Id` ([workspace.js `nameOf`](../fjs-prototypes/linear/web/src/lib/workspace.js)): one request per workspace, on every sign-in. (2) **The generated form's assignee picker offers only the caller.** The generated `<Issue />` lists exactly one row for `assigneeId` (Léa sees *Léa Martin*, Ana sees *Ana Ribeiro*), so no issue can be filed for somebody else through a generated form. linear's issue page reads `Membership.displayName` instead. (3) The admin who creates a private team is refused it on the next screen, and nobody can add its first member. That half is filed as [`FJS-1319`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1319), because the delegated tenancy rule is what refuses the membership row. (4) **The copy drifts, measured:** Ana renames herself on the scaffold's own user page (`User.name` → *Ana R. Ribeiro*, saved), and `Membership.displayName`, which every people picker reads, still says *Ana* in both her workspaces. Nothing derived connects the two. **Amended 2026-09-27, *Portal, the search stressor* Phase 1: a fourth site, a third spelling, and membership that depends on a STATE.** *Joe shares his golf lens with me* is a `Grant` row (`status` active → revoked by a named move), so the rule is *readable if an ACTIVE Grant names me and this lens* — `grants.any(granteeId == auth().id && status == 'active')` in the candidate spelling. Portal took neither linear answer: a copied `readerIds String[] @system @default([])` on `Lens`, read as `auth().id in readerIds`, and — **measured working** — through one hop from the child as `@@allow('read', auth().id in lens.readerIds)` on `DomainRule`, so the copy is read through a relation rather than copied twice. The cost is new to this row: the copy has to be rewritten by whatever drives `revoke`, so a revocation that forgets it leaves the friend reading, and nothing in the seed ties the move to the list. Phase 5 drives the revoke. **Amended 2026-09-27, *Portal, the search stressor* Phase 5: the revoke, driven.** (1) **The copy moves only where one service moves it.** `grants.service.ts` recomputes `readerIds` from the active grants after `create` and after `revoke`, in one transaction. Through that service, a revoke took Joe's lens out of my next search on the same token and the same terminal session (drive step 6, under a pty). Joe's sites in my top ten went 6 → 0, and my reads of the lens and its rules answered `[]`. **The same named move made on the model**, `system.grant.transition(id, 'revoke')`, which is what a job, a seed or `fli tinker` writes, answered `status: 'revoked'` and left `readerIds` naming me. My next search still folded in Joe's lens. That is pinned. The seed had to write the copy by hand beside its grants, and says so. `@@transitions` names the move and `transition-methods` checks that a method makes it, but nothing says what else the move changes, so the move and the copy it must rewrite live in two files that never meet. (2) **A fifth spelling that is not there: `@from`**, the one word that reads down a to-many relation. `shared Boolean @from(Pass, exists: true, where: …)` read by `@@allow('read', … \|\| shared)` is the natural shape. But `where:` is SQL text only: an expression (`status == 'active' && granteeId == auth().id`) is refused with *Expected STRING*. `auth().id` written inside the SQL string parses, and then breaks the parent's first plain `system` `create` with `SQLiteError: near "."`. That is pinned too. (3) **What the copy buys, measured:** because membership is a ROW and not a claim, a grantee's already-open socket is graded against it per frame. It heard Joe add a rule to the shared lens while the grant was active, and heard nothing (neither a patch nor a new rule) after the revoke. That is the freshness [`FJS-1316`](#fjs-1316)'s claim snapshot lacks. The other half is not fresh: the lens row itself stays in the grantee's open live list after the revoke, because the frame that narrows the read set is graded out rather than sent as *removed* ([`FJS-1425`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1425), measured in Chrome). [portal grants.service.ts](../fjs-prototypes/portal/api/src/services/grants.service.ts) · [portal trust.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/portal/api/test/trust.test.ts) (pinned) · [portal schema.lite `Lens.readerIds`](../fjs-prototypes/portal/db/schema.lite) · [portal-access.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/portal/api/test/portal-access.test.ts) **Amended 2026-09-29, jazzhr Phase 4 (Q5): a fifth site, and the first where the member list is the reader's OWN writes.** *An interviewer may not read the panel's scorecards until they submit their own* is `scorecards.any(interviewerId == auth().id && submittedAt != null)` over the parent. Measured refusals: `interview.scorecards.any(…)` is refused as *crosses two relations* (true of the path, but it names hops where the missing thing is a to-many test), and `exists(interview.scorecards, …)` does not parse. Spelled Portal's way: `Interview.submittedIds String[] @system`, read as `auth().id in interview.submittedIds`, written by the scorecards service's `submit` in one transaction. Works over HTTP and in Chrome, Firefox and the build: Ivy, having submitted, reads the panel; Hank reads only his own card until he submits, then Ivy's; Rita, on the panel with no card, reads `[]` and cannot learn how many others exist. Same cost as Portal's, pinned in [offer.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/api/test/offer.test.ts): a scorecard stamped `submittedAt` by the model alone (a seed, a job, `fli tinker`) leaves its author blind to their own panel. *The hiring manager reads every card* is a further two hops (scorecard → interview → application → job) and was not built: one more carried column |
| <a id="fjs-1280"></a>FJS-1280 | litestone · sierra | **`@@sync` is the word for a held WRITE, and it is what decides whether a model may be READ on a device — a direction its own `db:explain` says it has not been asked about.** | open | 2026-09-22 | Found building the offline half of connectteam Phase 7. `fli db:explain @@sync` says it in its own words: *The argument is the COLLISION policy and nothing else — **whether a model leaves the device, and in which direction, is a separate question this attribute has not been asked.*** And sierra refuses the read by name without it: *[Sierra] employees: offlineQuery needs @@sync on model Employee. Rows are only kept on a device for a model that declared it.* The reasoning behind that ([FJS-D298](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d298)) is right — writing rows to a device is a disclosure and wants a schema word, not a resource option — but the word it reuses is about something else, so an app that wants a roster readable in a basement must declare a collision policy for a model nothing ever writes offline, and **must pick one that is not true**: `append` says these rows are only ever added (an employment record is edited), `refuse` and `field` each demand an `@version` column on a model with no offline writer to be stale against, leaving `server` — *replay this against whatever the row holds by then* — as the least-wrong statement about a write that never happens. **Measured, three models declared for the read alone:** no DDL changed and no migration was needed, so the word costs nothing at the Data boundary; `fli check` went from 31 applicable rules to 32; and on `LeaveRequest` it put an **Id** box on an employee's *Ask for leave* form, because a client-mintable id on a syncable model becomes a column the client may write. Without the declarations nothing of her day was on the device at all: the app opened with no network and answered *Nothing scheduled for you today* for a published shift it had been shown an hour earlier. (The *not on the roster* line on the same screen was a different defect, [`FJS-1281`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1281).) A separate word for the read direction, or an argument on this one, would let a model say *held, never written* — which is what a reference table, a roster and a published schedule all are. · [`fli db:explain @@sync`](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [offline.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/offline.js) · [FJS-D298](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d298) · [FJS-D307](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d307) · blocked by `FJS-D488` |
| <a id="fjs-1271"></a>FJS-1271 | litestone | **A `File` ref stores the origin it was uploaded against, so every stored file points at the old host the moment an app moves — and the key that would survive the move is sitting in the same JSON.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Measured** while moving `example`'s drives off the dev ports. The ref in `product_image.file` is `{"key":"storage/ProductImage/2e6246fc4edd.png","provider":"local","endpoint":null,"publicBase":"http://localhost:8110",…}` — the key is relative and portable, and `publicBase` beside it is not. Start the same app on another port and every photograph 404s or refuses the connection while the bytes sit untouched under `storage/`; the drive that found it read `ECONNREFUSED 127.0.0.1:8110` from a browser whose API was answering on 7110. **A port is the cheap version of the real case, which is a DOMAIN**: an app that moves from `shop.example.com` to its own domain, or gets a CDN in front of storage, orphans every uploaded file in every row, and no migration can be written for it without parsing JSON in SQL. **Nothing reports it** — the rows read fine, the API answers, and only a browser fetching the url finds out. **What it costs today**: `example`'s drives cannot move the API port, which is why they move only the dev server. **The likely answer is that `publicBase` is DERIVED at read time** from the provider's current config plus `key`, which is § V.5 exactly — a stored copy of something the config already knows. Against it: an object genuinely served from a URL the current config no longer describes, which is an argument for keeping `endpoint` per row and not for keeping the app's own public base. **The derived path already exists and the stored value shadows it**: `urlFor` is *if a publicBase was stored in the ref, use it* ([storage/index.js:44](packages/litestone/src/storage/index.js)) and falls back to `endpoint` + `key` only when the ref carries none, so the smallest change is which of the two wins. It is written at upload in [file.js:234](packages/litestone/src/plugins/file.js). Needs a ruling before a change · found by `verify:cart` and `verify:catalog` |
