The drive builds before it asserts — deliberately, because a preview server pointed at a stale `dist/` reports on the previous fix — so the whole drive is dark until somebody either takes 4 kB back out or records the decision to pay for it. **Whose 4 kB is not established**: it was already over before this session's changes were involved, and the tree currently carries two sessions' uncommitted work, so attribution needs a build from a clean checkout rather than a guess. The gate itself is working as designed — this row is the growth, not the gate · [offline-shell.js](packages/sierra/src/postbuild/offline-shell.js) · [offline-baseline.json](example/web/offline-baseline.json) |
| <a id="fjs-1269"></a>FJS-1269 | notifications | **Nothing in the framework can reach a phone, and the stated reason for building it here — *a subscription is a row* — is the one thing an app already gets for free. The reason to build it is the 350 lines of RFC that every app otherwise writes identically and gets silently wrong.** | open | 2026-09-22 | Q5 of the connectteam stressor, answered ([PLAN.md § Q5](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/PLAN.md)). The brief refuses both shipped transports in one sentence — *if the reminder is an email nobody opens, or a badge inside an app that is not running, the person is late and we have paid for a system that did nothing* — so this product is the first on the list that is WRONG rather than lesser without push. **Ground state re-probed 2026-09-22:** `ls packages/notifications/drivers/` is `email.ts`, `inapp.ts`; `grep -rn 'vapid\|web-push' packages/ --include=*.ts --include=*.js` is **0 hits across the whole framework**. **What was free, and it is worth saying first.** The `transports:` seam cost nothing: anything with `send(recipient, message, app)` registers, a registered driver beats a built-in name, a transport with no driver is refused BEFORE any delivery rather than at the moment one is needed, and a `push:` formatter beside `inApp:` on a `defineNotification` needs no adapter. Nothing in the app is a workaround for this package. **What 2.17 and `ecosystem-gaps.md` § 16 both get wrong.** Both argue that making the subscription a Model *is the whole argument for building it here rather than leaving it to an app*, because it then inherits `@@gate`, `@encrypted` on the keys and Invariant 7's redaction, where *every hand-rolled version is a plain table with none of that*. **Measured: a hand-rolled version in a FrontierJS app is not a plain table.** `model PushSubscription` was declared in the app's own `db/schema.lite` and got all three — `@encrypted` hides `p256dh`/`authSecret` from every caller read and returns PLAINTEXT to `asSystem()`, which is what a sender needs and is the one of the five visibility words that fits; the audit trail carries `[redacted]` (see [FJS-1266](#fjs-1266) for the half of that which is a defect); and the gate is the app's to state. That argument is an argument for litestone, which the app already has. **What actually costs, measured.** `api/src/core/push.ts` is **~350 lines** implementing RFC 8291 (aes128gcm over ECDH-ES), RFC 8188 (the body framing) and RFC 8292 (VAPID): an ephemeral P-256 keypair per send, **four HKDF derivations** whose `info` strings must be byte-exact including their NULs, a `salt‖rs‖idlen‖keyid` header, the `0x02` last-record delimiter, and a JWT whose signature must be raw `r‖s` where node emits ASN.1 DER by default. **Every one of those fails the same way: the push service does not hold the key, cannot tell a bad derivation from a good one, and answers `201 Created`.** A wrong build is indistinguishable from a phone in a tunnel, which is why the app's own test decrypts what it encrypts with a device private key rather than asserting a length — every cheaper check passes on garbage. **Three more things an app must build that the driver interface does not suggest.** (1) **A driver that WRITES**: a subscription expires with no notice and the only signal is a 404/410 on a send, so the transport that delivers is the one that must prune; `transport`/`send`/`shutdown` hints at none of it. (2) **A fan-out inside the fan-out**: `inApp` writes one row per recipient, but a person is N devices with N key pairs and N independent 410s, and `Recipient` — whose design note is *a Recipient is not a User* — has nowhere to put them. (3) **`asSystem()` on every send**, because the keys are `@encrypted` and the sender reads everybody's rows, which is [FJS-1216](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1216)'s cost once more: every delivery is filed against the application rather than against anybody. **The boundary § 16 says to settle first, with a measurement.** *A push payload is a read that leaves the building via a vendor, so `@guarded` columns must not be in it.* It cannot be enforced at the driver: the driver necessarily holds `asSystem()` to decrypt the subscription keys at all, so it has full visibility by construction. The check belongs where the payload is BUILT — the notification's `push:` formatter — or nowhere. **Scope note:** measured to the edge, no vendor send, per that run's scope call. The request is built completely — URL, headers, VAPID JWT, encrypted body — and handed to an injected `deliver`; the default is a plain `fetch`, since a push service is ordinary HTTPS with no SDK and no account, which is what makes this buildable by an app at all. · `IDEAS/overview.md` 2.17 · `IDEAS/ecosystem-gaps.md` § 16 · [FJS-1270](#fjs-1270) · [FJS-1266](#fjs-1266) · [FJS-1234](#fjs-1234) · driven in `api/test/reminders.test.ts` |
| <a id="fjs-1270"></a>FJS-1270 | notifications · litestone | **`Notification.contextId` is `Int?`, so the paved road for telling somebody something cannot point at a row whose id is a ULID — and a ULID is the id shape litestone's OWN rule forces on any model a phone writes offline. The package's TypeScript says a string is fine.** | open | 2026-09-22 | Found building shift reminders on the connectteam stressor ([PLAN.md § Q5](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/PLAN.md)). `db/notification.lite` ships `contextId Int?` beside `contextType String?`, documented as *a loose reference to what the notification is ABOUT, with no foreign key by design*. This app's `Shift.id` is `String @id @default(ulid())` **because litestone's `sync-reference-to-a-server-assigned-id` rule refused an autoincrement** on a model a phone writes with no server reachable — so the framework pushes an app onto ULIDs with one rule and ships a notification column that cannot hold one with another. **Measured**, against the app's real schema with the shipped word put back: `notification.create({ …, contextType: 'Shift', contextId: '01K5Z9QW8YR3N4T6V7X8Y9Z0AB' })` → `cannot store TEXT value in INTEGER column notification.contextId`. A clean refusal rather than a silent truncation, which is the good half. **The bad half is that the package's own types allow it**: `NotificationRecord.contextId: number \| string \| null` and `InAppMessage.contextId?: number \| string \| null` ([types.ts](packages/notifications/types.ts)), so `inApp().context('Shift', shift.id)` typechecks, `fli check` is green, `bun run check` is green, and the refusal arrives at the first send. **It is load-bearing, not cosmetic.** A reminder sweep runs every five minutes over a 48-hour window, so it has to know what it has already sent — and `notification.lite` refuses a `sentAt` column by an explicit design note, correctly, because delivery is a transport concern and one row fans out to N transports. So *has this gone out* IS a query on `(type, contextId)`; with `Int?` the only place left for the id is `data`, which is `Json` and indexes nothing. The app widened the column to `String?` and `package-model-drift` reported it precisely — naming the column, both types and what to do — which is that rule working exactly as designed and is **not** part of this row. **What would close it:** `contextId String?` in the shipped fragment. A uuid or a ULID fits TEXT and an integer id fits it as digits, which is the same trade `User.accountId` in `auth.lite` already makes and documents in a comment. · [notification.lite](packages/notifications/db/notification.lite) · [types.ts](packages/notifications/types.ts) · driven in `api/test/reminders.test.ts` § 4 |
| <a id="fjs-1266"></a>FJS-1266 | litestone | **An `@encrypted` column reaches the audit trail two different ways depending on WHO wrote the row — `[redacted]` for a system write and ABSENT for everybody else's — so a trail reader cannot tell *the column was not supplied* from *it was supplied and you may not see it*.** | open | 2026-09-22 | Found on connectteam Phase 6 ([PLAN.md § Q5](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/PLAN.md)) and driven as a pair: two creates of one model with identical data, one through `asSystem()` and one as the ordinary USER(4) caller who owns the row. `model PushSubscription` declares `p256dh String @encrypted`, `authSecret String @encrypted` and `@@log(audit)`. The system write's `after` carries `"p256dh":"[redacted]"` and `"authSecret":"[redacted]"` — **Invariant 7, exactly as promised**. The owner's write has neither key in `after` at all. The trail is built from the row as the WRITER could read it back, and the visibility table's `@encrypted` row is *caller writes, caller does not read* — so for every principal below the system client the column is not redacted, it is missing. **Not a nulls-are-dropped artefact:** `userAgent`, a plain nullable column the same caller left unset, is in the same snapshot as `null`. The consequence is that the one question a trail exists to answer about a credential column — *was a device key written, or rotated, here* — has no answer. Absent means *not supplied* on the system path and *supplied, hidden* on every other, with nothing in the row to separate them, and a reader has no way to know which path wrote a given row. Invariant 7's promise is that such a column *logs as `[redacted]`*, which is a claim about presence as much as about the value. **What would close it:** build the snapshot from the row as WRITTEN and then redact, rather than from the row as read back. The redaction already exists and is already applied on the system path — this is which value it is applied to. · driven in `api/test/reminders.test.ts` § 3 · [FJS-D349](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d349) is the other direction of the same trail (a log that reveals too much) |
| <a id="fjs-1253"></a>FJS-1253 | litestone · sierra | **The model a picker points at and the model a person's NAME is on are routinely two models, and `@@label` takes one bare column of its own — so the fallback guess ran, picked a `@hashed` column, and the picker came back empty with a console warning.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Driven in a browser**, connectteam Phase 5. Every screen that assigns work picks an `Employee`, and an Employee's name is `user.name`, one relation away — the split is deliberate and was forced by the gate ladder ([FJS-1244](#fjs-1244)): an employment record is HR's, and the facts a person edits about themselves live on `User`. `@@label` is *a bare field NAME, like @@index and @@transitions take* — one column of the model itself — so *show the person's name* is not declarable, and the correct declaration for this model does not exist. What happens instead was measured: the kit guessed, warned `[shifts] options('employeeId') — showing Employee.kioskPin, the first plain string column, which is a guess`, and then **failed**: `options('employeeId') failed — Unknown or unsortable $orderBy key 'kioskPin' (stores a serialization — not sortable)`. `kioskPin` is `@hashed`: the read path strips it and the order path refuses it, so the picker for *which person is working this shift* rendered with no options at all, on a console warning and nothing else. Two halves. **The guess should skip a column the Data boundary will refuse** — `@hashed`, `@secret`, `@guarded` and anything unsortable are knowable from the same schema the guess is made against, and picking one produces an empty picker rather than an ugly one. **And the declaration should be able to reach one relation**, or a model whose display name belongs to a related row cannot declare it at all; every screen in this app that picks a person hand-writes the list instead, which is a second copy of a rule the schema should hold · [schedule/index.mesa](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/web/src/routes/schedule/index.mesa) · [field-rules.js:1156](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js) |
| <a id="fjs-1254"></a>FJS-1254 | sierra | **A `type` declared in `db/schema.lite` reaches the browser's own schema registry and the UI realm cannot address it: a Resource naming one silently renders the form for the SERVICE'S model instead — a different field list, and nothing warns.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Driven in a browser**, connectteam Phase 5. Every write in this app that matters is a custom method whose payload is not a row of any model: `shifts.assign` takes `type AssignShift` (the trio a manager types, who, where and an availability override) because the model's two instants are `@system` and three overlap rules run on the way in. The shape IS in the browser — `fli db:jsonschema` emits every `type` block into `$defs` beside the models, and `virtual:sierra` registers the whole table, confirmed by reading the dev server's own payload: `AssignShift` and `GeoFix` are in it. `createResource('shifts', { model: 'AssignShift' })` + `<Form>` then rendered **`Shift`'s** form: `siteId`, `employeeId`, `ruleId`, `onDate`, `startLocal`, `endLocal`, `status` — `ruleId` exists only on the model, `ignoreAvailability` only on the type. No warning from the resource, none from `<Form>`, and `fli check` green — `resource-model-miss` fires on a name that resolves to nothing, not on one that resolves to something else. **So the declaration a custom method is validated against on the server is invisible to the screen that fills it in**, and the labels, required flags and option lists get written out by hand in the page — the exact second copy the framework's one rule exists to prevent. Junction already takes `methods: [{ method: 'assign', input: 'AssignShift' }]` and validates the body against it (measured: a bad `onDate` comes back `400 [{ field, message }]`, rendered under the control), so the two ends of the same declaration disagree only about the browser. Either a Resource should be able to name a `type` and generate from it, or naming one should be refused by name · [schedule/index.mesa](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/web/src/routes/schedule/index.mesa) · [schema.lite § What a screen sends that is not a row](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/db/schema.lite) |
| <a id="fjs-1258"></a>FJS-1258 | litestone | **`createClient()` answers the hand-written `LitestoneClient`, whose every model is `unknown`, and nothing ties an app's generated `db/schema.d.ts` to it — so `db.employee` and `db.asSystem().employee` alike leave TypeScript, and the app's own `fli typecheck` gate fails on the lines the docs tell you to write.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Corrected 2026-09-27** by re-probe: the hand-written client's `asSystem()` already answers `LitestoneClient` ([index.d.ts:773](packages/litestone/src/index.d.ts)), not `AnyLitestoneClient`; a probe in connectteam gets `TS18046: 'db.employee' is of type 'unknown'` on the plain client too, so `asSystem()` is not the cause. connectteam's `core/db.ts` calls `createClient({ … })` untyped; `createClient<TClient>` exists but no doc or scaffold passes the generated type, and the generated `schema.d.ts` declares its own `createClient` that nothing imports. The fix is to make the generated types reach `db` (a scaffolded generic, or module augmentation), not to retype `asSystem()`. Original measurement, connectteam Phase 5: `db.asSystem().employee.findFirst({ … })` is `TS2571: Object is of type 'unknown'`, and spreading a row read that way is `TS2698: Spread types may only be created from object types` — two errors in the gate `bun run check` runs, from two ordinary uses of the one escape hatch the framework documents by name. The app's domain modules already carry `type Db = any` at every such boundary and the workaround here is another cast, which is the point: the bypass is not rare or discouraged — a seed uses it, a claim resolver uses it, every conflict check in this app uses it because a check must see rows the caller may not. `AnyLitestoneClient`'s comment says why it is shaped this way — a generated client declares its own `$schema` and naming a shape here would make the generated flavour unassignable ([FJS-018](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-018)) — so the fix is not to widen that interface but for `asSystem()` to answer the SAME type as its receiver, which is what it does at runtime. `db/schema.d.ts` is generated and holds exactly that type **Amended 2026-09-28, remnant (second caller, the service side)**: junction's `$.db` is the same hand-written `LitestoneClient`, so in a service `$.db.verse.findUnique({ … include })` answers `{}` and `fli typecheck` refuses every field read off it (14 errors in one service). The app's generated `db/schema.d.ts` reaches neither. remnant states the shapes it reads and casts, in [verses.service.ts](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/api/src/services/verses.service.ts) and [search.service.ts](../fjs-prototypes/remnant/api/src/services/search.service.ts) · [index.d.ts:525](packages/litestone/src/index.d.ts) · [core/auth.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/src/core/auth.ts) · blocked by `FJS-D495` |
| <a id="fjs-1260"></a>FJS-1260 | cli | **`fli make:service <Model>` writes a service that contradicts junction's own documented shape on four counts — and its plural disagrees with `fli make:resource`'s, so the two generators for one model produce a page that 404s.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Driven**, connectteam Phase 5, nine generated services. The template is `const plural = lower + 's'` and emits `export default createBaseService({ name: plural, model: plural, hooks: {} })`, against a package whose AGENTS.md says the file is `<plural>.service.ts` exporting `create<Plural>Service()` over a PascalCase singular `model:`, and against the scaffold's own `users.service.ts`, which is written that way. Four disagreements: `export default` where the autoloader documents a factory (*a `*.service.ts` exporting no `create…Service` factory is an authoring error that refuses `start()`* — it does not, measured: the app booted with all nine); `model: 'sites'` where the word is `Site`; a file named for the singular; and printed next steps naming `api/src/server.ts` and `app.services.register(…)`, neither of which exists in a `fli new --full` app, where services are autoloaded from `junction.config.js`. **The plural is the one with a cost.** `fli make:service ClockEntry` serves `/api/clockEntrys`; `fli make:resource ClockEntry`, run five minutes later, writes `createResource('clockEntries')` and tells you the service file is `clockEntries.service.ts`. Measured: `GET /api/clockEntries` → `404 Service 'clockEntries' not found`, `GET /api/clockEntrys` → `200`. Two generators, one model, one letter apart, and the failure lands in the browser rather than at either command **Re-measured 2026-09-28, remnant**: given the service's own name, as junction's file naming reads it (`fli make:service verses`), it writes `name: 'versess', model: 'versess'`, and still prints the `api/src/server.ts` next step. Not re-filed · [make/service.md:38](packages/cli/commands/make/service.md) · [junction AGENTS.md](packages/junction/AGENTS.md) **linear, 2026-09-22 (Phase 2), a third app:** `fli make:service Issue` wrote `issue.service.ts` holding `export default createBaseService({ name: 'issues', model: 'issues' })` and printed *Register in api/src/server.ts* — a file this scaffold does not have, since services are autoloaded; `fli make:resource Issue` then named `issues.service.ts` for the same model. Rewritten by hand as `issues.service.ts` in the scaffold's `createIssuesService()` shape, likewise `comments` and `teamMembers`. |
| <a id="fjs-1248"></a>FJS-1248 | junction · litestone | **The audit trail has the exact column pair for *a device attested this, on behalf of that person* — `actorId` plus `subjectId` — and there is no route to it for an attester that is not a User holding a live Session, so a shared-kiosk write is filed as though the person did it alone.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Measured** in the connectteam stressor, PLAN.md § Q4 — a wall tablet, forty clock-ins a morning, a four-digit PIN. **The seam opens and nothing had to be worked around to open it**: `app.runAs(employee.userId, fn)` re-resolves the principal through `auth.sessionFor()`, and a clock-in from a device holding no session lands attributed to the right person at the standing they hold NOW, readable by them under `ClockEntry`'s own row policy (driven). **What has nowhere to go is the attestation.** `auditLogs` already carries `actorId`, `actorType`, `subjectId` and `episodeId`, and the doc comment on them states this exact case: *filing an impersonated write under the impersonated person is the default everywhere else and is what makes a trail unusable as evidence — the person who did it is the one who is missing from it.* A hallway tablet is that case with a device where the operator would be. **Two routes exist and neither is reachable.** (1) `session.support`, set by the auth provider — `auth.startSupport(token, subjectId, reason)` requires the OPERATOR to hold a live `Session` row and refuses self and chaining, and a `KioskDevice` is not a `User` and has no session by construction, which is the entire premise of the question. (2) `db.$logContext(fn)`, which litestone calls when it builds an entry — but it is ONE SLOT on the ROOT client, its own comment says *one app-wide install at boot, not a per-caller capability*, and junction claims it (`installLogContext`) to supply `correlationId`, `source`, `origin`, `ip`, `userAgent` and `tenant`. An app installing its own replaces junction's for the whole PROCESS rather than for one request, so attesting one clock-in would stamp that device on every concurrent write in the API and drop five provenance columns while it did. `app.runAs` itself takes a user id and `{ tenant }` and nothing else. **So the attestation survives only as DATA**, in `ClockEntry.deviceId` — a column this application invented, which `@@log(audit)` then snapshots into `after`. Said precisely, because *the device is missing from the trail* is the easy claim and is wrong: the tablet's id is in the trail, and it is not in a column any trail reader understands. `actorId` and `subjectId` are indexed and queryable across every model; a payload column is an app-private convention that only this app's code can interpret, on the one model that happens to carry it. *Which acts did this tablet attest, across every model, last Tuesday* is answerable from the first pair and not from the second — and a leave request approved at the same tablet has nowhere at all, because adding the column to every model an attested act could touch is what a declaration exists to avoid. **The second half is the standing.** There is no way to say *this principal was proved by four digits*: `sessionFor` hard-codes `authMethod: 'created'`, and the columns the ladder grades are facts about the USER rather than about how this call authenticated. Driven: inside the kiosk's own `runAs` scope the tablet renames the person on their `User` row, because `@@gate("4.4.4.5")` plus `@@allow('update', id == auth().id)` is the same rule for a password and for a PIN. The pair is measured beside it — what DID narrow the scope was a row policy about whose row it is, which would have refused a password too. The alternative, `db.$setAuth` with a hand-built narrowed principal, buys the narrowing and loses what `runAs` exists for: the standing stops being re-resolved, so somebody disabled this morning still clocks in. **Whether the answer is a third argument to `runAs`, an `attestedBy` on the provenance closure, or a composable `$logContext`, is the open design question** · [app.ts § runAs](packages/junction/src/core/app.ts) · [litestone.ts § installLogContext](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [client.js § buildLogEntry](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [kiosk.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/src/domain/kiosk/kiosk.ts) · [identity.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/identity.test.ts) · blocked by `FJS-D490` |
| <a id="fjs-1249"></a>FJS-1249 | litestone | **A credential a SERVER verifies has no word: `@secret` cannot be matched in a `where`, `@hashed` is refused beside it by name, and `@secret(deterministic: true)` keeps the plaintext recoverable — so a device token is decrypted and compared in application JavaScript, timing safety included.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Measured** in the connectteam stressor, PLAN.md § Q4, on `KioskDevice.token @secret` — a wall tablet's own credential, the thing that makes a PIN entered in a hallway worth anything. `@secret` expands to `@encrypted` plus `@guarded` plus logged, which is right about everything except the one operation a credential exists for. Looking it up throws, and the refusal is exact and names both alternatives: *the column holds ciphertext under a random IV, so no plaintext can ever equal it and this filter matches nothing. Use `@encrypted(deterministic: true)` if the value must be both looked up and read back, `@hashed` if it only ever needs matching.* **Neither is what a server-verified credential wants.** `@hashed` is the correct storage and is REFUSED beside `@secret` by name at [parser.js:5283](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) — *`@hashed` conflicts with `@secret` — `@secret` implies `@encrypted` and `$rotateKey`, and a digest can do neither* — which is a true statement that costs the rotation and the access log `@secret` was chosen for. `@secret(deterministic: true)` is matchable and leaves the plaintext recoverable by anything holding the encryption key, which is the property a credential is stored one-way to avoid. **So the third thing is what an app does**, and it is what this one does: read the row through `asSystem()`, which lifts `@guarded` and decrypts (driven — `asSystem()` reads the token back verbatim), then compare with `node:crypto` `timingSafeEqual`. The device token is therefore in plaintext in the API process on every clock-in, and the comparison's timing safety is the application's problem. **The contrast is one model over and it is the whole row**: `Employee.kioskPin @hashed` needs none of this — matchable in a `where` because the client encodes the operand the same way the column was encoded, stripped from every read including `asSystem()` because an HMAC has no inverse, and a `select` naming it throws rather than handing back a digest that looks like a value. Two credentials in one flow want the same treatment and only one of them can have it. The missing word is *one-way, rotatable-by-reissue, and logged* — `@secret` minus the reversibility, or `@hashed` plus the audit half · [parser.js § @secret expansion](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [kiosk.ts § authenticateDevice](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/src/domain/kiosk/kiosk.ts) · [identity.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/identity.test.ts) · blocked by `FJS-D478` |
| <a id="fjs-1244"></a>FJS-1244 | litestone | **The gate ladder cannot express a drop box — *many may write, few may read* — unless the reader is the SYSTEM. `@@gate("6.4.9.9")` is refused; `@@gate("8.4.9.9")` boots.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Measured** against `createClient` on a one-model schema, read position varied over a create of 4: **5, 6 and 7 are refused** (*levels must be non-decreasing in R.C.U.D order*), **8 and 9 boot**, because the sentinels do not advance the ladder. So the shape is available exactly when no person may read the rows, and unavailable for every human standing. **The product shape this refuses is ordinary**: an anonymous staff survey, a suggestion box, an incident report, a whistleblower form, a job application — anyone may submit one and only HR reads them. The connectteam stressor hit it on `SurveyResponse` (any employee creates, level 6 reads), which is the model [`IDEAS/field-workforce.md`](IDEAS/field-workforce.md) § Open questions is about. `PushSubscription` in the same schema has the same shape — the browser registers its own, only the sender reads — and is legal **only** because the sender is the system, so it lands on read 8 by luck rather than by choice. **The workaround is the thing the framework teaches against.** Demote the gate to the create level and restate the read rule as `@@allow('read', auth().isAdmin)` — which moves a *what kind of caller* rule into the *whose row* position, and converts a refusal into a silent filter: a caller who may not read responses gets an empty 200 rather than a 403. `fli explain @@gate` states the split in the opposite direction (*a gate REFUSES — it throws naming the model and the level, where a policy filters*). **The rule's own stated rationale is about a different pair.** `fli explain @@gate`: *levels must be NON-DECREASING … because a model easier to delete than to read is a mistake every time.* That argument is about delete against read; it is applied to create against read as well, where the drop box is the standing counter-example. Whether the monotonic rule should be per-pair rather than global is the ruling this row wants. Found by the connectteam stressor, `fjs-prototypes/connectteam/PLAN.md` Phase 1 (Q3). **The predicted cost of the workaround was driven in Phase 4, 2026-09-22, and it is worse than a 403 becoming a 200.** With the read rule demoted to `@@allow('read', auth().isAdmin)`, HR reading a round with one response gets one row and an ordinary employee reading the SAME round gets `[]` — no error, no model name, no level. The two answers a caller cannot distinguish are *you may not read these* and *nobody has answered yet*, and in a staff survey those are the two readings that matter: the second is the one that makes somebody conclude the consultation failed. A gate would have said which. Driven in [identity.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/identity.test.ts), the refusal beside the caller who is allowed the same read. This is also the row [FJS-1247](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1247) is built on top of: the model whose gate could not be written is the model whose anonymity cannot be declared either · [identity.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/identity.test.ts) · [FJS-1247](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1247) · blocked by `FJS-D491` **Amended 2026-09-29, over HTTP (jazzhr Phase 3, `.probe/phase3/portal.sh`).** An applicant tracker is the drop box, and its applicant is a second reader the ladder has no position for either: strangers write `Candidate` (`@@gate("0.0.0.5")`, read 0 and the policy says who), recruiters read it, and the person who wrote it reads their own row through a link. `GET /api/candidates` and `GET /api/applications` with no credential → **200 `{"data":[],"total":0}`**, byte-identical to what a link holder sees for somebody else's row and to what a forgotten applicant's dead cookie gets — the silent 200 this row predicts, on the public internet. The same caller with a redeemed link → 200 with their one row. So the drop box has THREE readers — nobody, the owner by claim, staff by standing — and the gate can grade only the third; the other two are policy, and a refusal is impossible to spell for the first · [jazzhr PLAN.md § Q3](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/PLAN.md) |
| <a id="fjs-1242"></a>FJS-1242 | cli | **`fli check` runs its rules over a schema litestone itself refuses to parse and exits 0 — so `bun run check` is green on a schema that cannot migrate and kills the app at boot.** | open | 2026-09-27 | **Measured** in the connectteam stressor on one line — `kioskPin String @hashed @guarded` on a model, which litestone refuses with *@hashed conflicts with @guarded*. Against that file: `litestone validate` errors, `litestone migrate create` refuses, and the app's own boot throws (`_buildEnv`, `testing.js:141`). `fli check` prints `⚠ 31 rule(s) checked · 9 warning(s)` and **exits 0**, saying nothing — it reads the schema well enough to run `transition-methods` over every move in it. `fli check --list` holds 61 rules and none asserts that the schema parses: `schema-in-memory` grades whether the committed artefacts describe the running models, which is a different question and passes here. **The only thing that failed `bun run check` was the scaffold's generated `api/test/access.test.ts`**, which calls `parse()` for its own reasons; with that one file replaced by a trivial passing test, the whole gate exits 0 — 31 rules, lint clean, typecheck clean, 1 pass. Every scaffold's `AGENTS.md` names `fli check` as the Data-realm gate and `bun run check` as *the same gate CI runs*, and the generated `ci.yml` runs only that. Same family as [FJS-1228](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1228) — the gate does not read the UI realm either — one realm over, and this is the realm `fli check` exists for. **A second instance, a different class of error and a worse ending.** `@@gate("4.6.5.6")` violates the ladder's non-decreasing rule. Parse does not catch it — the gate is validated at plugin init — so `litestone migrate create` wrote a migration and `fli db:migrate` **applied it**, creating 23 tables for a schema `createClient` then refuses with *levels must be non-decreasing in R.C.U.D order*. `litestone validate` catches it and exits 1, because it is the one tool that runs both passes; `fli check` exits 0 again. So the honest statement of this row is not *`fli check` misses one rule* but **there is no single command in the gate that answers "will this schema run"** — and the one that does (`litestone validate`) is not in `bun run check`, not in the generated `ci.yml`, and is named in no scaffold's `AGENTS.md`. Adding it to the `check` script is a one-line fix. Found by the connectteam stressor, `fjs-prototypes/connectteam/PLAN.md` Phase 1. **Third instance, 2026-09-27, *Portal, the search stressor* Phase 1** — a third class: `@@unique([ownerId, lensId, domain])` over an optional `lensId`, which litestone refuses as *cannot be enforced* (and names the fix). `fli check` exits 0 (33 rules, 2 warnings); `fli advise` and `createClient` refuse; `bun run check` went red only through the scaffold's `access.test.ts` calling `makeTestClient` — the same single thread. **And the one-line fix this row proposes is the wrong line, measured:** `litestone validate` is *which STORED rows this schema would now refuse* — it opens `db/app.db`, so on a schema ahead of the database (every change before its migration) it exits 1 with *no such table* per new model, and with no `app.db` (CI; it is gitignored) it creates an empty one and exits 0. The command that answers *will this schema run* without a database is `fli advise`: exit 1 on the refusal above, exit 0 on the fixed schema with warnings. Portal's `check` script now opens with `fli advise &&` ([package.json](../fjs-prototypes/portal/package.json)). |
| <a id="fjs-1237"></a>FJS-1237 | litestone | **A validator whose type the column cannot hold parses clean, so `@gte(1)` on a `Boolean` reaches the read schema as `{"type":"boolean","minimum":1}`.** | open | 2026-09-21 | Found beside [`FJS-1236`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1236) and kept apart from it, because that row is about a rule that can never be REACHED and this is about one that can never be MEANT. **Measured**, each parsing `valid: true` with no error and no warning: `flag Boolean @gte(1)`, `name String @gte(1)`, `n Int @length(2, 5)`. The first reaches a generated form as a numeric minimum on a checkbox. `@@fts` over an encrypted column and a fractional `@default` on `@scale` are both already ruled — the first in `advise.js`, the second in the parser — so the question is which side of [`FJS-721`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md)'s boundary this falls: nothing SQLite refuses, so `advise.js`, unless the pairing is decidable enough to be a parse error. The catalog already states each validator's argument shape (`core/catalog.js`), so the type it applies to is derivable rather than a second hand-written table. **One neighbour of this is now answered and it narrows what is left**: a value rule on a RELATION is `value-rule-on-a-relation` in `advise.js` ([`FJS-1239`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1239)), which leaves the scalar-against-scalar mismatch as the whole of this row. |
| <a id="fjs-1229"></a>FJS-1229 | litestone · sierra · ui | **A column whose value a create policy DETERMINES (`hostId == auth().id`) is rendered by the generated form as a free choice over every row of the related model — so the form a scaffold ships, submitted by the person it is for, is refused by the schema.** | open | 2026-09-21 | Reported by a user of the calendly stressor, who opened *New event type*, filled it in and pressed Create: **`Create denied by @@allow policy on "EventType"`**. The schema is right and the form is wrong. `EventType` declares `@@allow('create', hostId == auth().id)`, which says there is exactly ONE legal value for that column and the caller is it. The generated form reads `hostId` as an ordinary required relation and draws a picker over every `User` — *Showing 88 of 133 — type to search the rest* — defaulted to the first row, which is somebody else. **The default state of a generated create form is a guaranteed 403.** **The information does not survive the trip.** In `db/schema.json` the model carries `x-gate`, `x-relations`, `x-identify` and `x-litestone-kind` — and its policies as `x-litestone-policies: true`, a BOOLEAN ([jsonschema.js:776](packages/litestone/src/jsonschema.js)). `hostId` itself crosses as `{"type":"string"}`, with nothing saying it is determined rather than chosen. So sierra's field-rule resolver could not do better with what it is given: the fact is written in `.lite`, compiled into SQL, and flattened to a bit on the way to the browser. **The framework's own escape hatch is in direct tension with the form.** The scaffolded service carries `before: { create: [() => { if (!data.hostId && $.me) data.hostId = $.me.userId }] }` — it fires only when the field is ABSENT, so it is defeated by the very form the same generator writes. **And hiding the field is not enough** — corrected 2026-09-21 after the same user hit the next wall. The resource's `validate` step runs in the BROWSER, `hostId` is in the model's `required` list, and the client cannot know a `before` hook is about to supply it — so a form with the field merely removed refuses to submit at all, reporting ***host is required*** for a request the server accepts. Measured both directions on two models (`EventType`, `AvailabilitySchedule`): `POST` without `hostId` → created with the caller's id; the same payload through the resource → refused before it left the page. The working shape is `record` and `except` TOGETHER — seed the value, hide the control — which is what the framework's own generated exceptions form already does for `scheduleId` and which nothing documents as the pattern. So an app must do two undocumented things per determined column, and neither `fli check` (34 rules) nor `fli advise` mentions either. **The shape of a fix is small and there are two halves.** Carry the constraint: a policy whose expression is `<column> == auth().<claim>` is a derivable fact, and emitting it beside `x-gate` — `x-determined: { hostId: "auth().id" }` — is what the rest of the row already does for gates and relations. Then `<Form>` drops the field and the client supplies the value, which also removes the need for the hook. Until then `fli advise` could name it: a required relation column that a create policy pins to `auth()` is exactly its *legal but wrong* remit. A near neighbour worth checking in the same pass: the same form offered a `scheduleId` picker over 144 rows belonging to every host, because `AvailabilitySchedule` had no read policy — a second way the generated form proposes values the write will refuse · [jsonschema.js:776](packages/litestone/src/jsonschema.js) · [field-rules.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js) · [Form.mesa](packages/ui/components/forms/Form.mesa) · the stressor's `web/src/routes/event-types/create.mesa` · blocked by `FJS-D492` |
| <a id="fjs-1230"></a>FJS-1230 | ui | **`slot="name"` is honoured by exactly one component in the kit and silently dropped by every other — so a `<Card>` given a header and a footer renders neither, and the screen looks finished rather than broken.** | open | 2026-09-21 | Found when a user of the calendly stressor reported a dead end on the Availability screen. The real damage was one layer past it: **that page had lost both of its headings AND both of its forms**, and nothing anywhere said so — no warning, no console message, no build error. The add-window form and the add-exception form simply were not in the DOM, so the screen read as an empty state that had been designed. [Card.mesa](packages/ui/components/layout/Card.mesa) declares `header` and `footer` as snippet props and renders `{@render header?.()}`; **it references `$slots` zero times**, so `<h2 slot="header">` and `<div slot="footer">` are dropped. [Form.mesa:928-932](packages/ui/components/forms/Form.mesa) does both — `{@render actions?.()}` `{:else if $slots.actions}<slot:actions />` — and is **the only component in `packages/ui/components` that does**; measured across every component declaring a snippet prop, `Form` is 1 and all the rest are 0. **The kit already knows this is a real trap, and fixed it in one direction only.** Form's own comment: *Two spellings because two protocols reach a component and the rest of this kit uses the other one: every other container here (Card, Table, EmptyState, SectionHeader) takes its trailing content as a snippet prop, so a caller who wrote `{#snippet actions()}` over a form got no buttons and no complaint.* That is this bug with the operands swapped, it was worth a code change when it pointed at Form, and the general case was left open. **Apps are actively taught the dropped spelling.** Every Resource `fli scaffold` generates ends with `<Button slot="actions" …>` inside a `<Form>` — correct, because Form is the exception — so a developer reads `slot=` as the kit's idiom and writes `<h2 slot="header">` on the next Card they reach for. That is exactly the path this app took. **This is [FJS-1208](#fjs-1208)'s class met a second time**: content handed to a component and discarded with no diagnostic. That one was `<EmptyState>` dropping children across 28 call sites in basecamp; this one is every container in the kit dropping named slots. The narrow fix is the `$slots` fallback Form already has, copied to Card, Table, EmptyState and SectionHeader. **The better fix is the one neither has: warn.** A component that receives `$slots.<name>` it will not render knows, at render time, that it is throwing content away — and a `console.warn` naming the component and the slot would have turned a silent blank screen into a one-line answer. Nothing else catches it: `fli check` (34 rules) and `fli typecheck` are both green over the broken page, and Biome cannot read `.mesa` at all ([FJS-1228](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1228)) · [Card.mesa](packages/ui/components/layout/Card.mesa) · [Form.mesa:928](packages/ui/components/forms/Form.mesa) · [FJS-1208](#fjs-1208) · the stressor's `web/src/routes/availability/index.mesa` |
| <a id="fjs-1223"></a>FJS-1223 | notifications | **The paved road for telling a person something cannot carry an attachment, while the transport underneath it can — so a confirmation with an `.ics` has to leave the notifications package entirely.** | open | 2026-09-20 | Three layers and the middle one is the wall. Junction's `MailMessage` declares `attachments?: MailAttachment[]` and `smtp.ts` builds correct multipart MIME for it, `Content-Disposition: attachment` and all ([mail/index.ts:27](packages/junction/src/mail/index.ts), [smtp.ts:565](packages/junction/src/mail/smtp.ts)). Notifications declares its OWN `MailMessage` — `{ subject, lines, to?, html?, text? }`, no `attachments` — its builder offers `subject/greeting/line/action/html/text` and no `attach()`, and `sendEmail` calls `app.mail.send({ to, subject, text, html })`, so an attachment would be dropped even if the authoring type grew a slot for it. **The two types are hand-kept in sync and the file says so**: *Keep in sync with junction's `MailMessage` in `src/mail/index.ts`* ([types.ts:121](packages/notifications/types.ts)). That is the same duplication whose last divergence was [FJS-904](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md) — a subject with no body, every `.greeting()` dropped, and the send reporting success — and the note beside it records exactly why: *notifications declares its own structural `App.mail` type rather than importing junction's, so the two can disagree.* They disagree again, now on `attachments`. **For a scheduling product this is the artifact the whole thing is for**: the calendly stressor sends its confirmation through `app.mail.send()` directly, which works and which means the notification kind, its preferences and its in-app sibling are all bypassed to attach one file. The fix is either an `attach()` on the builder plus a passthrough in the driver, or notifications importing junction's type instead of restating it · [drivers/email.ts](packages/notifications/drivers/email.ts) · [builders.ts](packages/notifications/builders.ts) · [types.ts](packages/notifications/types.ts) |

A double that took a message SMTP would refuse is worse than no double: the test passes and the send fails in production (`FJS-904`). One owner for the rule, in `smtp.ts`; this is the door to it.* **The door is not in the wall.** [index.ts:112](packages/junction/index.ts) exports `createResendMailer`, `createSmtpMailer`, `mailerPlugin`, `createMessage` and `MailBuilder`, and the type line beside it exports `IMail`, `MailMessage`, `MailAttachment` and `SendResult` — none of the three asserters. Measured: `assertMessageAddresses` is `undefined` on the root import, and the package's `exports` map has only `.`, `./litestone` and `./testing`, so there is no subpath to reach around by either. The calendly stressor writes an `IMail` capture double for exactly the stated reason and had to restate the address rule by hand, badly — one owner became two at the first app that took the advice. | <a id="fjs-1213"></a>FJS-1213 | litestone · cli | **`verifyGateLadder` returns *this gate is wrong* and *this gate could not be asked* in one array, and the documented assertion — the one `fli new` generates into every app — is that the array is empty. Declaring what `AGENTS.md` tells you to declare on line one turns it red.** | open | 2026-09-27 | `docs/testing.md` shows `expect(await env.verifyGateLadder()).toEqual([])` and the scaffold ships exactly that in `api/test/access.test.ts`. **Measured on a five-model scheduling schema: 43 rows, 0 gate defects.** 16 are `skipped` — a create policy the synthetic principal cannot satisfy, which the same doc's own table describes as correct behaviour; 19 are `error` from one cross-column `@@check` (`startTime < endTime`) the auto-factory cannot satisfy, reported honestly as *no fixture could be built, so the gate was never asked* and still counted; 8 are [FJS-1212](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1212). The app's only ways out are to stop declaring row policies, to delete the `@@check`, or to write an exclusion list against a tool whose job is to be trusted — the third is what this one did, with the ids in the comment. **This is [FJS-1006](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1006)'s principle with new causes** — *a checker that emits 27 unaskable rows is one people stop reading* — and that fix was per-cause (views filtered to `read`), so each new cause re-opens it. The general shape is two channels: the mismatches, which must be empty, and the non-gradings, which an app should be able to see, count and hold steady. A `{ mismatches, ungraded }` return, or an `ungraded: 'throw' \| 'return'` option, would let the generated assertion stay honest and stay green · [testing.js](packages/litestone/src/testing.js) · [docs/testing.md](packages/litestone/docs/testing.md) · [FJS-1006](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1006) **Second app, 2026-09-22 — connectteam, and it gives the row a number.** A 14-model workforce schema, none of it exotic, turns the generated assertion red with **346 entries: 342 say the gate was never asked, 4 are labelled mismatches, and 0 are access holes** — the auto-factory cannot build a fixture for a model carrying `@point`, `@money` or a `String @date`, which is most of them. The 4 labelled as mismatches are miscategorised rather than real: each carries `"thrown": "Validation failed — this record is not valid"` beside `"expect": "allow", "got": "error"`, so the classification INSIDE the array is wrong too, not only the assertion over it. An app author's only reading of `bun test` on day one is *my schema has 346 access problems*. **Third app, 2026-09-22: linear, the first under `strategy row`, and a new cause.** 62 entries, 0 access holes. 19 are one cross-column `@@check` (`Cycle`, `startsAt < endsAt`), the same cause as the first measurement, so `Cycle`'s gate is never asked at all. 43 are `skipped`, and under row tenancy that number is structural: the tenancy block desugars a `@@deny('create', …)` onto EVERY scoped model, so each scoped model's allowed-create cells are unaskable whether or not the app wrote a policy. linear's [access.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/linear/api/test/access.test.ts) filters on why an entry is present, as connectteam's does **Amended 2026-09-27, *Portal, the search stressor* Phase 1: 29 rows, 0 gate defects, and two causes this row did not have.** 24 are `skipped` (create/update policies, as above). **4 are `error` on `Bang.create`, *FOREIGN KEY constraint failed***: `ownerId String? @system @default(auth().id)` stamps the SYNTHETIC principal's id into a foreign key to `User`, and no such user exists — [FJS-1200](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1200) fixed exactly this for `verifyTenantIsolation` (a sentinel) and the ladder was not given the same fix. **1 is `error` on `Grant.update` at SYSTEM, *granterId is @immutable***: the ladder's update fixture writes every column, `testing.js` has no mention of `@immutable`, so any model with an immutable column fails its update rung at the one level no policy skips. Portal's `access.test.ts` now filters `skipped` and these two by model and op, with the ids in the comment — the third way out, again. | <a id="fjs-1245"></a>FJS-1245 | litestone | **A per-move `@gate` on `@@transitions` is a FLOOR on the model's `update` level and can only raise it, so a named move can never be more permissive than an arbitrary edit — and *anybody submits their own document, only a manager approves it* is not expressible.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Measured** in the connectteam stressor: `Timesheet @@gate("4.5.5.7")` with `@@transitions(status, submit: open -> submitted, approve: submitted -> approved @gate(5) @seals, …)`. The employee submitting their own timesheet is refused with `"Timesheet.update" requires level 5, user has level 4` — although `submit` declares **no gate at all**, which at a call site reads as *no extra authority required*. It is not: `gradeMove` is reached only after the update gate has passed, and the comment there states the intent — *a transition gate is a floor on top of `@@gate`'s update level* ([client.js:3695](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js)) — with a rationale (*shipping an order and refunding one are not the same authority*) that argues only for RAISING. **The direction that works was measured beside it**: `approve @gate(5)` correctly refuses USER(4) and is taken at ADMINISTRATOR(5). **The shape that has no spelling is a move-grain drop box**, and it is common rather than exotic: submit a timesheet, acknowledge a policy, hand in a form, request a review, mark a task done. Each is *many people may make THIS ONE move, and almost nobody may edit the document*. The only two settings available both lose: leave `update` at 5 and the person cannot submit their own work, which is what this app shipped; or drop `update` to 4 and any signed-in caller may PATCH any field of anybody's timesheet, narrowed only by a row policy that would then have to be written for every operation. **`@@capabilities` is not the escape** — a named move is a capability automatically, but the grid is ANDed with the gate rather than substituted for it, so the ladder still refuses first. **This is [FJS-1244](#fjs-1244)'s finding on a second mechanism.** That row is the CRUD ladder refusing *many write, few read*; this is the transition ladder refusing *many make one move, few edit*. Two different words, one missing idea: the framework can express *more authority than an update* and cannot express *this specific act, by anyone who may read the row*. The fix is a design question rather than a patch — whether a move's `@gate` should REPLACE the update level rather than floor it, and what `fli check` should say about a move that lowers **Amended 2026-09-28 by the jazzhr stressor (Phase 1), measured, and it moves the row.** *"`@@capabilities` is not the escape"* holds only for a steep gate: on a FLAT one ([`FJS-D146`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d146)'s own advice) the grid IS the move-grain drop box. `Application @@gate("0") @@capabilities`: a bearer holding `['Application.withdraw']` withdraws, and is refused `reject` by name; `Offer @@gate("0") @@capabilities` with `signedName @capability`: the candidate signs and accepts, is refused a salary edit (`Offer.update`), and staff holding no `Offer.accept` cannot accept for them. Three costs remain. **(a)** Without the grid the ladder forces the whole model down: `"0.4.0.5"` (a stranger's move, staff-only create) is refused as *update below create*, so every staff-only verb becomes a silent policy. **(b)** A grid model a stranger must also CREATE cannot exist: [FJS-1486](#fjs-1486). **(c)** The grid is per person, so *only THIS job's hiring manager may approve* has no spelling: a move takes a level or a grant, never a row predicate · [jazzhr schema.lite § Offer](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/db/schema.lite#L594) · [client.js § gradeMove](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [schema.lite § Timesheet](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/db/schema.lite) · [hours.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/hours.test.ts) · [FJS-1244](#fjs-1244) · blocked by `FJS-D511` **Amended 2026-09-29, jazzhr Phase 4: *only THIS job's hiring manager may approve* is spellable today, measured over HTTP.** The offer carries its job (`jobId Int @immutable`, held equal to `application.jobId` by the create policy `jobId == application.jobId`, so a one-hop column and never a stale copy), and `@@deny('post-update', status == 'approved' && job.hiringManagerId != auth().id)` grades the row the write would leave. A recruiter holding `Offer.approve` is refused by name (403 *Update denied by @@deny post-update policy on "Offer"*), the job's hiring manager approves, `send` and `withdraw` out of `approved` are unaffected, and litestone's own `transitions()` reports `approve: false` for the recruiter. Two costs, both stated: it is a rule about a STATE, not a move (exact only because `approve` is the only way into `approved`), and it must be a DENY: a second post-update ALLOW, added for a different rule, ORs with the first and re-admitted the recruiter (caught in review, not by a test). The direct spellings are each refused at parse with a good message: `application.job.hiringManagerId` *crosses two relations … carry the value on this model*. What stays open here is the ladder dragging create to 0 · [jazzhr `.probe/phase4/approver.ts`](../fjs-prototypes/jazzhr/.probe/phase4/approver.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-1246"></a>FJS-1246 | example | **`example`'s payroll has no input shape for a MEASURED hour: `annualGross` is `rate * hoursPerWeek * 52` and `hoursPerWeek` is an `Int` contracted constant, so a workforce product's whole output can reach a payslip only by being averaged back into the contract it was measured to replace.** | open | 2026-09-22 | **Graded, not ported** — PLAN.md § Q6 in the connectteam stressor, against the shapes `example/api/src/domain/payroll/` demands and with nothing copied into that tree. An approved timesheet (`@@transitions`, `@seals`, lines carrying `onDate`, `kind`, `minutes` and a `rateSnapshot` frozen at the rate in force on the day) was converted into `PayWindowRow` and run through transcribed copies of `annualGross` and `periodShare`. **It goes, and everything that makes it a measurement is lost on the way.** Spring fortnight: 420 measured minutes, **€84.00 actually earned**, → `{ basis: 'hourly', rate: 1200, hoursPerWeek: 4 }` → `annualGross` €2496.00 → `periodShare(·, 26)` **€96.00 — out by €12.00**. Autumn fortnight: 540 minutes, €108.00 earned → `hoursPerWeek: 5` → €3120.00 → **€120.00, out by €12.00**. **Four refusals, and only the fourth is one a type checker catches.** (1) `hoursPerWeek` is an `Int @gte(0) @lte(168)`, so a fortnight holding one 7-hour night is **3.5 h/week and is stored as 4** — the rounding alone is the whole €12.00. (2) `PayWindow` holds ONE `rate` for a window while a timesheet holds a `rateSnapshot` per LINE, so a raise mid-period is a fact about a day at one end and a fact about a window at the other, and neither shape can express the other without losing the split. (3) `PayComponentKind.overtime` is in `EARNING_KINDS` and `draftPayslip` emits exactly one earning line, always `basicPay`; `overtime` is reachable only through `arrears.ts`, which is a CORRECTION to a previous period — so a timesheet that HAD overtime lines would have nowhere to put them. The record's own observation (*an enum value nothing in the application produces*) measured from the other side, and the timesheet builder cannot produce one either, because overtime is a rule about a WEEK and no row in either schema holds one. (4) `employeeId: number` against a uuid. **The loop is therefore not closable at this joint**, and the reason is a modelling one rather than a bug: `example` prices a CONTRACT and a workforce product establishes a MEASUREMENT, and the only bridge available re-derives the contract from the measurement and then pays the contract. Whether the fix is a third basis (`measured`, whose lines ARE the gross), or a `Payslip` that can be drafted from lines rather than from a window, is the open question. Nothing here is a defect in what `example` ships — it is the input shape being the one thing that was taken on faith · [employment.ts § annualGross](example/api/src/domain/payroll/employment.ts) · [payslip.ts § draftPayslip](example/api/src/domain/payroll/payslip.ts) · [handoff.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/src/domain/payroll/handoff.ts) · [hours.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/hours.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-1214"></a>FJS-1214 | litestone | **[`FJS-D143`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d143) ruled on 2026-08-25 that a column declares what KIND of time it holds, and nothing implements it — so the first product that needs a recurring wall-clock time cannot spell its central object.** | open | 2026-09-20 | **Measured**: `grep -rn "timeZone\|timezone" packages/litestone/src --include=*.js` → **0 hits**, no `zoned`, no `wallClock`, and no import of `@frontierjs/toolbelt/datetime`. The catalog's only time words are validators — `@date`, `@datetime`, `@time`, all in the *Refuse a bad value* group — so *Tuesdays 09:00–17:00 in Europe/Lisbon* is stored as `String @time` with the zone in a sibling column on another model and the relationship between them held by a comment. **Corrected 2026-09-20 after measuring the browser payload — the gap is narrower and sharper than first filed.** The FORMAT half already works end to end: `@time` crosses as a `pattern` plus `x-time`, and sierra's control resolver renders `<input type="time">` for it, in a branch whose own comment reads *a wall clock has no zone, so `<input type="time">` round-trips it and the plain input is right* ([field-rules.js:619](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js)). So the framework already holds the wall-clock CONCEPT in three places — that resolver, toolbelt's `fromWall`/`resolveWall`, and `FJS-D143` itself — and the schema language is the one place that cannot say it. What is missing is the ZONE BINDING: nothing declares that this column resolves against `schedule.timeZone`, so the pairing is convention held by a service that remembers, no query can ask *is this instant inside this window* (the zone resolution SQLite cannot do is the same one `FJS-D143` measured as a silent NULL), and the comparison lives in application JS — where this app has already written it wrong once, applying its time check to one branch of a two-branch function. **The arithmetic is not what is missing** — `@frontierjs/toolbelt/datetime` already exports `fromWall`, `resolveWall`, `partsIn` and `plainDateIn` with Temporal's `disambiguation` vocabulary, which is the twenty lines the ruling scoped. What is missing is the declaration that binds a column to a zone, and a comparison the framework computes and binds. A plain DATE has the same gap wearing the other half of the vocabulary (`AvailabilityOverride.date`, *not the 24th*), so one word plausibly carries both. Found building the calendly stressor, where it is the reason the exercise was chosen · [`IDEAS/time-and-recurrence.md`](IDEAS/time-and-recurrence.md) · [catalog.snapshot.md](packages/litestone/catalog.snapshot.md) · [datetime.js](packages/toolbelt/src/datetime/datetime.js) · [schema.lite § AvailabilityRule](../fjs-prototypes/calendly/db/schema.lite) **Priced in the connectteam stressor, 2026-09-22 — the answer is money, in both directions, and the two errors cancel.** *Tuesdays 22:00–06:00, Europe/Lisbon* at €12.00/h, driven across both of Lisbon's 2026 boundaries. The wall clock the manager typed is **480 minutes on every night of the year**, because it cannot see a boundary. The night starting Sat 28 March is **420 minutes** (`2026-03-28T22:00Z` → `2026-03-29T05:00Z`, offset 0 → +60); the night starting Sat 24 October is **540** (`2026-10-24T21:00Z` → `2026-10-25T06:00Z`, offset +60 → 0). So the same rota line pays **€96.00 both nights against €84.00 and €108.00 actually worked — overpaid €12.00 in spring, underpaid €12.00 in autumn**. **The two errors cancel over a year, which is what makes this worse than a one-directional bug**: an annual total reconciles, a year-on-year comparison reconciles, and nothing audits to anything. One person, one weekly night shift, on a rota of sixty. **The layer that should have owned the difference is the COLUMN.** `startLocal String @time` says it holds a time and not which zone reads it, so the app resolves the instant pair itself and stores it in two `@system` columns — and **nothing in the language knows those two columns were derived**. Measured: a depot entered as `Europe/Lisbon` that is really in the Azores, corrected afterwards to `Atlantic/Azores`, leaves every stored `startsAt` **one hour out, permanently, with nothing anywhere disagreeing** — and because both zones observe DST the error is CONSTANT rather than seasonal, so even the comparison that would have caught the DST version cannot see it. **And the validator half is missing too, which is how the wrong zone got in.** `Site.timeZone String @default("UTC")` accepted `"banana"` without complaint and threw at the first shift, from `@frontierjs/toolbelt/datetime` — one layer below the one that should have refused the write. The IANA set is knowable at parse time (`Intl.supportedValuesOf('timeZone')`), so `@zone` is a validator that could exist beside `@date`, `@datetime` and `@time` and does not. Calendly's answer to the same gap was *a slot cannot be spelled*; this one is *a person was paid the wrong amount, twice, and the two amounts hide each other* · [hours.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/src/domain/timesheet/hours.ts) · [wall.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/src/domain/scheduling/wall.ts) · [hours.test.ts](../fjs-prototypes/connectteam/api/test/hours.test.ts) · blocked by `FJS-D512` |
| <a id="fjs-1211"></a>FJS-1211 | repo · litestone | **Five of `PHILOSOPHY.md` §V's nine questions have a generated artefact to answer them from, and the two about VOCABULARY are not among them — so *is this a new concept* is answered from memory on every proposal that asks it.** | open | 2026-09-20 | Measured over the 28 committed snapshots the `snapshots` CI phase gates. **Five questions have one and it is direct**: *derived* has the derivations themselves (`ddl`, `jsonschema`, `access`, `release`, `routes`, `principal`, `jobs`, `notifications`), *owner* has [seams.snapshot.md](seams.snapshot.md), *boundary* has [exports.snapshot.md](exports.snapshot.md) plus `surface` and `principal`, *failure* has [errors.snapshot.md](packages/junction/errors.snapshot.md) and `access`, *silence* has [invariants.snapshot.md](invariants.snapshot.md). **Two are partial and they are the front half.** *Origin* has nothing that lists where each truth lives — `seams` lists handoffs, which is not the same claim. *Concept* has [catalog.snapshot.md](packages/litestone/catalog.snapshot.md), every word a `.lite` file can hold with its completeness asserted against the parser — and **no other realm has an equivalent**; the honest shapes are either that §2 becomes generated and the authored claim moves down to the places that enforce it, or that the dictionary derives only what has an enforcer and cites §2 for the rest, marking a term with no enforcer the way `invariants.snapshot.md` marks a `none` row. | <a id="fjs-1208"></a>FJS-1208 | ui | **`<EmptyState>` renders `title`, `description` and the `action` snippet and DROPS everything else between its tags — so twenty-eight usages in basecamp write an explanation into a component that shows none of it.** The component's markup has no children slot at all ([EmptyState.mesa](packages/ui/components/display/EmptyState.mesa)): `{#if title}`, `{#if description}`, `{#if action}`, and nothing renders the default block. Every call written as `<EmptyState title="Never released">This app has no deployment history.</EmptyState>` draws the title and loses the sentence, which is the half that says what to do about it — the exact failure the component's own header warns against (*an empty state without a way out is a dead end*). Nothing reports it: a dropped child is not a warning in Mesa, and a screen with a heading over blank space reads as a designed empty state. The fix is a children slot rendered between the text and the actions, and then a sweep of the call sites — 28 of the 30 usages in `packages/basecamp/web/src` have a closing tag. Found building the inline-source editor, where the only way out of the empty state was a button that never appeared · [FJS-D345](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d345) | open | 2026-09-20 | Two of them are not text but ACTIONS — a button written as a child renders nothing and the empty state has no way out, which is how this was noticed. Grading it is a spec in `packages/ui`'s own drive: render an `<EmptyState>` with a child and assert the child is in the DOM. |
| <a id="fjs-1209"></a>FJS-1209 | orion · toolbelt | **The flow expression's TEXT grammar is a subset of what the engine runs: thirteen forms compile and seven parse, so `template`, `array`, `object`, `pipe`, `let` and `match` can be stored and never written as a line.** A backtick is even tokenized as `TK.TEMPLATE` and read by no parser, so a template literal is a token with no grammar behind it. | open | 2026-09-20 | Measured over every flow definition in this repo: **427 of 471 expression nodes have a text form**, and the 44 that do not are 15 templates, 15 objects, 6 pipes, 5 arrays and 3 lets. `expressionToText` answers `{ text: null, reason }` for them and the inspector shows the document, which is honest and is why this is S3 rather than a blocker. **The two that would pay for themselves are `template` and `object`**, because they are what a `model.create`'s `data` and an `ai` prompt are built from — a title assembled from a literal and a `$.` path is the commonest thing a flow writes and is the one shape an author most wants on one line. The cost is that the grammar is **`@frontierjs/toolbelt/predicate`, which litestone parses `.lite` policies with**, and one language is the whole of [`FJS-D271`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d271) — so a template literal in a flow expression is a template literal in an `@@allow`, which needs its own ruling and a second interpreter in `compileSql`. That is the question, not the syntax: does the shared language grow a value-assembling form, or does orion accept that the assembling shapes are documents. Filed rather than decided, and it is the same question `IDEAS/orion-port.md` § 25/26 are waiting on an owner for. · [emit.ts](packages/orion/src/engine/expression/emit.ts) · [parse.js](packages/toolbelt/src/predicate/parse.js) `value` · [tokenize.js](packages/toolbelt/src/predicate/tokenize.js) `TK.TEMPLATE` · [FJS-1198](#fjs-1198) · blocked by `FJS-D513` |
| <a id="fjs-1205"></a>FJS-1205 | litestone | **The browser host writes a named refusal for `@encrypted`/`@hashed` on a device and it can never be the error a caller sees — `Buffer.concat(…)` is evaluated as the ARGUMENT to the refusing function, so the throw is `ReferenceError: Buffer is not defined`.** | open | 2026-09-20 | `src/host/browser.js` exports `createHmac` as `no('createHmac', KEYS)`, whose message is the whole explanation a device developer gets: *an @encrypted or @hashed column is not readable on a device: WebCrypto is asynchronous, and holding the key here would defeat the attribute.* Every call site in `core/encryption.js` builds its arguments first — `createHmac(HMAC_ALG, Buffer.concat([Buffer.from(key), salt('kid')]))` — and `Buffer` is node's, absent in a browser, so the argument throws before the refusal is reached. **The refusal is the design and it is right** (`FJS-D305`); what is broken is that the sentence explaining it is unreachable, which is the *advice that fails when taken* class `proof-target` names. **`deviceSchema` is the likelier layer for the fix**: it already grades what it drops `changed`/`lost`/`noted`, and it says nothing about `@encrypted` today — a device handed a column it cannot read is a column that should have been filtered, with a note, rather than one that throws at the first write. | <a id="fjs-1198"></a>FJS-1198 | orion | **The canvas is not built, and a React mockup is still the only statement of it.** A flow's definition has no graph surface: nodes and edges are added by editing JSON on `flows/[flowId].mesa`. The per-node inspector under it is built. | open | 2026-09-20 | **The server half is finished**, which is what makes this purely UI work: `FlowLayout` is a real model and `flows` answers `layout` / `saveLayout` ([services.ts](packages/orion/src/services.ts)), beside `activate`, `pause`, `archive`, `restore`, `run`, `dryRun`, `export`, `import` and `versions`. The specification is `packages/orion/mockup/ui/components/` — `flow-editor.jsx` (2,752 lines: pan, zoom, drag, edge routing) and `nodes.jsx` (2,365: fourteen node config panels, the webhook test panel, cron presets, the trigger context editor, the credential picker, the expression field), plus `NODE_CONFIG_FIELDS` and `schemaToFields` in `node-types.js`. **`@frontierjs/ui` has no graph component**, so the canvas is the expensive part and the kit has nothing for it. **The mockup does not run** — no `package.json`, no vite config, not a workspace member and in no `nonMembers` allowance — so it is read, never driven, and the drag cannot be felt before it is built; the seven files the port superseded were deleted 2026-09-16 so that what remains is exactly what is owed. Until 2026-09-20 this was recorded only in `PROJECT_STATE.md` § What is not and `IDEAS/orion-port.md` § UI, neither of which `fli next` reads **Measured 2026-09-20, and it splits the work in two rather than one.** The AUTHORING surface today is one control — `web/routes/flows/[flowId].mesa` line 191 is a 16-row `<Textarea>` holding raw JSON, which is the whole of how an operator writes an automation. **The inspector is largely DERIVABLE and should not be ported**: all 25 built-in node types already declare a `configSchema` (`src/engine/plugins/builtins.ts`), and the framework already owns JSON Schema → controls — `controlFor`, `formFieldList`, `<Form>` and `registerControl` ([FJS-D17](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d17)). The mockup contains BOTH paths and prefers the wrong one: `schemaToFields` is a hand-rolled four-branch type map, and `resolveNodeFields` consults a hand-written `NODE_CONFIG_FIELDS` table FIRST and falls back to the schema only for plugin nodes — so most of `nodes.jsx`'s 2,365 lines are a table the descriptors already carry. What is genuinely owed there is a few contributed controls: the expression field, the credential picker, cron presets and the webhook tester. **The canvas is the part nothing shrinks** — 2,752 lines of pan, zoom, drag and edge routing with no kit component under it. So the row is one real build (the canvas) plus a generated form, not 5,100 lines of port. **The inspector half landed 2026-09-20 and the row is now the canvas alone.** It is 300 lines against the mockup's 2,365 and it writes no field table: `flows.nodeTypes()` carries the registry's descriptors to the browser — the REGISTRY's, so a host's plugin node is in it, which is the only reason `configSchema` can be on a plugin manifest — and `buildFieldRules` + `formFieldList` turn each one's `configSchema` into controls. What the port could not have derived is that **a config value is never a plain value**: the resolver dispatches on the stored node's own `type`, so `model: 'Customer'` is `{ type: 'literal', value: 'Customer' }` and the same slot may hold a `ref`. The schema describes what the value must RESOLVE to and the stored node is the expression around it, so a field has two states and the stored value says which — which is also why 17 properties across the built-ins declare no `type` at all. A computed value is still edited as its document ([FJS-1206](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-1206)). Proved by `verify:automations`, 56 assertions from 37, and by `test/node-forms.test.ts`, which grades every built-in's schema through the same pipeline so a property no control can render fails the suite rather than appearing as an empty box. · [PROJECT_STATE.md](packages/orion/PROJECT_STATE.md) · [orion-port.md](IDEAS/orion-port.md) · blocked by `FJS-D510` |
